# 第2步：题目原文、来源与数据边界审核——Person A工作包

## 简介

逐题确认题面、证明、来源、数据划分和使用权利，解决转录、解释、选择、注入错误与泄漏问题。

本工作包分配给 **Person A**，共 **304 道题**。只完成本文件不足以关闭该步骤；必须与另一人的工作包合并、比较分歧并完成必要裁决。

## 本步要求

1. 逐字核对仓库版本和原始来源。
2. 检查假设、量词、定义域、公式排版及参考证明对应关系。
3. 判断歧义、代表性、重复／泄漏、许可与发布用途。
4. 注入错误样本必须比较注入前后版本。
5. 语义影响修订由另一人复查。

## 公共记录

- 验证批次编号：________
- 分支：________
- 提交 SHA：________
- 对象摘要或范围清单：________
- 审核者：________
- 审核角色：________
- 审核时间：________
- 证据目录：________
- 已知限制：____________________________________________________________________

## 执行纪律

1. 逐项独立判断，不复制系统预测或另一审核者答案。
2. 机器结果只作定位线索，不构成数学、语义、权利或发布正确性证据。
3. 不确定时填写“不确定”或“需修订”，不得猜测通过。
4. 保留原始意见；复核与裁决不得覆盖初次记录。
5. 每个对象都必须填写结论、理由和证据路径，空白对象视为未审核。

### 1. m2-001

- 数据组：M2 工程 Pilot
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m2/source/pilot_50.jsonl`
- 领域：`elementary_algebra`
- 来源地址：未提供

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
证明两个偶整数之和为偶数。
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：- x、y 为偶整数。

证明：- {"node_id": "n1", "text": "存在整数 m,n，使 x=2m，y=2n。"}
- {"node_id": "n2", "text": "于是 x+y=2m+2n=2(m+n)。"}
- {"node_id": "n3", "text": "因为 m+n 是整数，所以 x+y 是偶数。"}
```

#### 机器验证结果（不代表人工通过）

- JSON 记录已成功解析

### 2. m2-003

- 数据组：M2 工程 Pilot
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m2/source/pilot_50.jsonl`
- 领域：`elementary_algebra`
- 来源地址：未提供

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
证明对任意整数 n，n^2+n+1 是奇数。
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：- n 为整数。

证明：- {"node_id": "n1", "text": "相邻整数 n 与 n+1 中至少有一个是偶数，所以 n(n+1) 是偶数。"}
- {"node_id": "n2", "text": "因此存在整数 k，使 n(n+1)=2k。"}
- {"node_id": "n3", "text": "于是 n^2+n+1=n(n+1)+1=2k+1，所以 n^2+n+1 是奇数。"}
```

#### 机器验证结果（不代表人工通过）

- JSON 记录已成功解析
- 源文件 SHA-256：`7f10d1ecf2627f326402580e47055496b3a0041aef1a8e25f374e79ce85f8a0e`
- 逐题稳定外部来源地址：未提供

### 51. opc250-001

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`usamo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
I have an \(n \times n\) sheet of stamps, from which I've been asked to tear out blocks of three adjacent stamps in a single row or column. (I can only tear along the perforations separating adjacent stamps, and each block must come out of the sheet in one piece.) Let \(b(n)\) be the smallest number of blocks I can tear out and make it impossible to tear out any more blocks. Prove that there are real constants \(c\) and \(d\) such that

\[
\frac{1}{7} n^{2}-c n \leq b(n) \leq \frac{1}{5} n^{2}+d n
\]

for all \(n>0\).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Proof.  We think of the final “blocked’’ configuration as the result of tearing out \(b(n)\) disjoint 3-stamp blocks (each block either 3 in a row or 3 in a column) so that no further 3-stamp block remains.  Call any three consecutive positions in a row or in a column a “potential triple.’’  There are in all  
\[
H \;=\; n\,(n-2)
\quad\text{horizontal triples,}
\qquad
V \;=\; n\,(n-2)
\quad\text{vertical triples,}
\]
and hence 
\[
T \;=\;H+V \;=\;2\,n\,(n-2)
\]
potential triples that must each be “hit’’ (i.e.\ each must lose at least one stamp) by our removed blocks.

1.  Lower bound \(b(n)\ge \tfrac1{7}n^2-cn\).  
 
Fix any one of the \(b(n)\) removed blocks.  If it is a horizontal block occupying cells \((i,j),(i,j+1),(i,j+2)\), then it “hits’’ (i.e.\ destroys at least one cell of) at most
  •  all horizontal triples that include one of those three cells;  in a row of length \(n\), each interior cell lies in 3 horizontal triples and each end‐cell in 1 or 2, and one checks easily that the three consecutive cells \((i,j),(i,j+1),(i,j+2)\) together lie in at most 5 distinct horizontal triples;  
  •  all vertical triples that include one of those three cells;  each of the three removed stamps lies in at most 3 vertical triples, so altogether at most \(3\times3=9\) vertical triples.  
Hence that one horizontal block can destroy at most \(5+9=14\) of the \(T\) potential triples.  Likewise any vertical block destroys at most \(14\) potential triples.  

Since in the final configuration every one of the \(T=2n(n-2)\) potential triples must be destroyed by at least one of the removed blocks, and since each block can destroy at most \(14\) triples, we get the inequality
\[
14\,b(n)\;\ge\;2\,n(n-2)\;=\;2n^2-4n,
\]
i.e.
\[
b(n)\;\ge\;\frac{2n^2-4n}{14}
\;=\;\frac1{7}\,n^2-\frac{2}{7}\,n.
\]
Thus the stated lower bound holds with \(c=\tfrac{2}{7}\).

2.  Upper bound \(b(n)\le \tfrac1{5}n^2+dn\).

We give an explicit “tiling’’ construction with density \(1/5\), up to an \(O(n)\) boundary correction.  Assume for simplicity that \(n\) is a multiple of 5; the leftover strip of width \(<5\) on the right and top can then be handled by at most \(O(n)\) extra blocks without changing the leading term.

Partition the \(n\times n\) grid into \(\tfrac n5\times\tfrac n5\) little \(5\times5\) macrosquares.  In each \(5\times5\) macro with local coordinates \((r,c)\), \(1\le r,c\le5\), remove exactly the following 5 blocks (each of size 3):

  A)  A horizontal block in row \(r=2\), columns \(c=1,2,3\).

  B)  A horizontal block in row \(r=4\), columns \(c=3,4,5\).

  C)  A vertical block in column \(c=2\), rows \(r=3,4,5\).

  D)  A vertical block in column \(c=4\), rows \(r=1,2,3\).

  E)  A horizontal block in row \(r=5\), columns \(c=2,3,4\).

One checks by an easy (though a bit tedious) case‐by‐case verification that in this repeated pattern every horizontal triple of three consecutive stamps in any row will contain at least one removed stamp (from one of the above horizontal blocks in its macro or in an adjacent macro), and similarly every vertical triple of three consecutive stamps in any column meets one of the vertical blocks C) or D).  Thus after tearing out these \(5\) blocks in each \(5\times5\) cell there is no triple left anywhere in the \(n\times n\) square.

Since there are \(\tfrac n5\) macros in each direction, the total number of blocks used is
\[
5\;\times\;\Bigl(\frac n5\Bigr)^2
\;=\;\frac15\,n^2.
\]
Adding the \(O(n)\) extra blocks needed to cover the final partial strips (of width \(<5\)) along the top and right edges gives
\[
b(n)\;\le\;\frac15\,n^2 \;+\;O(n).
\]
Hence the upper bound holds with some constant \(d\).

Combining (1) and (2) completes the proof: for all \(n>0\),
\[
\frac1{7}\,n^2-\frac{2}{7}n\;\le\;b(n)\;\le\;\frac1{5}\,n^2+d\,n,
\]
as required.  ∎
```

### 52. opc250-003

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`izho`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
The numbers \( a_{1}, a_{2}, \ldots, a_{100} \) are a permutation of the numbers from \( 1 \) to \( 100 \). Let \( S_{1}=a_{1},\ S_{2}=a_{1}+a_{2},\ \ldots,\ S_{100}=a_{1}+a_{2}+\ldots + a_{100} \). What is the maximum number of perfect squares that could be among the numbers \( S_{1}, S_{2}, \ldots, S_{100} \)?
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Answer.  The largest possible number of perfect squares among  
\(S_{1},S_{2},\dots ,S_{100}\) equals  

\[
\boxed{60}.
\]

The proof is divided into two parts.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
1.  A permutation which produces 60 squares
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Write first the 50 odd numbers in increasing order and afterwards the
50 even numbers in a suitable order:

\[
\begin{aligned}
(1,3,5,\dots ,97,99,           &100,54,50,98,70,44,96,68,56,88,80,60,\\
                               &94,76,66,92,74,78,90,72,62,28,86,82,  \\
                               &52,40,84,64,58,42,38,36,30,34,32,26,   \\
                               &24,22,20,18,16,14,12,10, 8, 6, 4, 2,48,46).
\end{aligned}
\]

(The exact order of the last 24 even numbers is irrelevant; any ordering
of the still unused even numbers works just as well.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Why this order works
--------------------------------------------------------------------

1.  The first term is \(1\), hence \(S_{1}=1^{2}\).  
    For \(k=1,2,\dots ,50\)

    \[
        S_{k}=1+3+\dots +(2k-1)=k^{2},
    \]

    because the first \(k\) summands are the first \(k\) odd numbers.
    Thus each of \(S_{1},S_{2},\dots ,S_{50}\) is a perfect square;
    that already gives 50 squares.

2.  After the first 50 numbers the partial sum equals
    \(50^{2}=2500\).
    The remaining 50 numbers are all even, so every further increment is
    even and every later partial sum is even as well.  
    Between \(2500\) and the final total \(5050\) the even perfect
    squares are

    \[
        52^{2},54^{2},56^{2},58^{2},60^{2},62^{2},64^{2},66^{2},68^{2},70^{2},
    \]
    i.e. 10 numbers.  
    The order written above was chosen so that the successive blocks

    \[
    \begin{aligned}
      &(100,54,50),     &&(98,70,44),\\
      &(96,68,56),      &&(88,80,60),\\
      &(94,76,66),      &&(92,74,78),\\
      &(90,72,62,28),   &&(86,82,52,40)
    \end{aligned}
    \]

    have respective sums  

    \[
      204,\;212,\;220,\;228,\;236,\;244,\;252,\;260,\;268,\;276,
    \]
    which are exactly the successive differences  

    \[
        52^{2}-50^{2},\;54^{2}-52^{2},\;\dots ,\;70^{2}-68^{2}.
    \]

    Consequently  

    \[
      S_{53}=52^{2},\;S_{56}=54^{2},\;\dots ,\;S_{100}=70^{2},
    \]

    and the ten numbers
    \(52^{2},54^{2},\dots ,70^{2}\) all appear among the partial sums.
    Altogether the permutation produces \(50+10=60\) perfect squares.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
2.  Proof that 60 squares are the maximum
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Two simple facts are needed.

(a)  There are exactly 50 perfect squares not exceeding
     \(2500 (=50^{2})\), namely \(1^{2},2^{2},\dots ,50^{2}\).

(b)  Among the 21 perfect squares which are larger than 2500 but not
     larger than \(5050\) only 10 are even:
     \(52^{2},54^{2},\dots ,70^{2}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Upper bound for the small squares
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Obviously at most 50 of the numbers
\(S_{1},\dots ,S_{100}\) can lie in the interval \([1,2500]\),
because there are only 50 squares in that interval.
So no more than 50 ​small​ perfect squares can appear.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Upper bound for the large squares
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Remove from the permutation all 50 odd numbers.
Every odd number changes the parity of the running sum,
hence it separates the sequence of even numbers into at most
51 blocks.  
Inside any single block the partial sums change only by even
amounts, so they are all even.  
Therefore every perfect square which occurs inside one block must be
even as well.

Once the running total exceeds \(2500\) all later partial sums belong
to one of those blocks (because all odd numbers are already spent) and
are even.
Because of (b) there are only 10 even squares above 2500,
hence not more than 10 of the large squares can occur.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Putting the estimates together
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Combining the two bounds,

\[
   \#\{\text{perfect squares among the }S_{k}\}
   \le 50 \;+\; 10 \;=\; 60 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Conclusion
--------------------------------------------------------------------

A permutation producing 60 perfect squares was exhibited,
and no permutation can produce more.
Consequently the required maximum equals 60.
```

### 53. opc250-005

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`baltic_mo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \( X \) be a subset of \(\{1, 2, 3, \ldots, 10000\}\) with the following property: If \( a, b \in X, a \neq b\), then \( a \cdot b \notin X\). What is the maximal number of elements in \( X \)?
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To solve the problem, we are given a set $ X \subseteq \{1, 2, 3, \ldots, 10000\} $ with the property that for any two **distinct** elements $ a, b \in X $, the product $ ab \notin X $. Our goal is to determine the **maximum possible size** of such a set $ X $.

---

### Key Observations

1. **Condition Clarification**:
   - The condition only applies to **distinct** pairs $ a, b \in X $.
   - The product $ ab $ must **not** be in $ X $, but it **can** be outside the set $ \{1, 2, \ldots, 10000\} $, which is acceptable.
   - There is **no restriction** on the product $ a \cdot a $, i.e., squares are allowed in $ X $.

2. **Strategy**:
   - To **maximize** the size of $ X $, we aim to include as many elements from $ \{1, 2, \ldots, 10000\} $ as possible while satisfying the given condition.
   - A natural idea is to consider a **threshold** $ N $ such that all elements $ \geq N $ are in $ X $, and all elements $ < N $ are excluded.
   - The goal is to choose the **smallest** $ N $ such that **no product** of two **distinct** elements in $ X $ lies in $ X $.

---

### Constructing the Set

Let’s consider the set:
$$
X = \{100, 101, 102, \ldots, 10000\}
$$
This set contains all integers from 100 to 10000, inclusive.

- **Size**: The number of elements is $ 10000 - 100 + 1 = 9901 $.
- **Verification**:
  - For any two **distinct** elements $ a, b \in X $, we have $ a, b \geq 100 $, so $ ab \geq 100 \times 101 = 10100 > 10000 $.
  - Therefore, $ ab \notin X $, satisfying the condition.

This construction works and is valid.

---

### Can We Do Better?

Suppose we try to include some **smaller numbers** (i.e., $ < 100 $) into $ X $. For any such number $ s < 100 $, and for any $ l \in X $, the product $ s \cdot l $ is at least $ 2 \cdot 100 = 200 $, and thus could lie within the range $ [100, 10000] $.

- **Example**: If we include $ s = 2 $, then $ 2 \cdot 100 = 200 \in X $, which violates the condition.
- **General Case**: For any $ s < 100 $, the product $ s \cdot l $ for $ l \in X $ may lie in $ X $, **requiring removal** of those elements from $ X $.
- **Cost of Inclusion**: Including a small number $ s $ forces us to **remove all elements in $ X $ that are multiples of $ s $**, which can be many (e.g., for $ s = 2 $, we'd remove about 4950 elements from $ X $).
- **Net Effect**: The number of elements we must remove far exceeds the number we can add, resulting in a **net loss** in size.

Thus, **adding any element below 100** leads to a **decrease** in the size of $ X $, making the original set $ \{100, \ldots, 10000\} $ the **maximal** such set.

---

### Final Conclusion

The set $ X = \{100, 101, \ldots, 10000\} $ satisfies the condition that for any two **distinct** elements $ a, b \in X $, the product $ ab \notin X $. This set has exactly $ 9901 $ elements, and any attempt to include additional elements from below 100 results in a **net decrease** in the size of $ X $.

---

### Final Answer

$$
\boxed{9901}
$$
```

### 54. opc250-007

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`putnam`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
A-2 Alice and Bob play a game in which they take turns removing stones from a heap that initially has $n$ stones. The number of stones removed at each turn must be one less than a prime number. The winner is the player who takes the last stone. Alice plays first. Prove that there are infinitely many $n$ such that Bob has a winning strategy. (For example, if $n=17$, then Alice might take $6$ leaving 11; then Bob might take $1$ leaving 10; then Alice can take the remaining stones to win.)
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Here is a complete proof.  We think of the game as an impartial “subtraction” game with subtraction‐set  
\[
S \;=\;\{\,p-1: p\text{ is prime}\} \;=\;\{1,2,4,6,10,12,16,18,\dots\}.
\]
A position (heap of size) $n$ is called an  N–position if the next player to move can force a win, and a P–position if the previous player (i.e.\ the opponent of the player to move) can force a win.  In the usual theory one shows:

 1.  $0$ is a P–position.
 2.  A position $n>0$ is N exactly if it can move to some P (i.e.\ $\exists\,s\in S$, $s\le n$ with $n-s$ a P).
 3.  A position $n$ is P exactly if **every** move takes it to an N (i.e.\ for all $s\in S$, $s\le n$, the position $n-s$ is N).

We shall exhibit *infinitely many* P–positions.  We do so by the standard “greedy” construction of P–positions for a (possibly infinite) subtraction game.

Definition.  Let
\[
a_1 = 0,
\]
and having chosen
\[
a_1 < a_2 < \cdots < a_{k-1},
\]
define
\[
a_k
\;=\;
\min\Bigl\{\,n>a_{k-1}:\;n - a_j\not\in S\text{ for all }1\le j<k\Bigr\}.
\]
In words, we pick the next P–position to be the least integer bigger than the last one which is *not* at a subtraction‐distance in $S$ from *any* earlier P–position.  We will show:

(i) This process never gets stuck, so produces an *infinite* strictly increasing sequence
\[
0 = a_1 < a_2 < a_3 < \cdots.
\]

(ii) Exactly the set $\{a_k\}$ it produces is the set of P–positions of our game.

From (ii) it follows at once that there are infinitely many P–positions, i.e.\ infinitely many $n$ for which Bob (the *second* player) has a winning strategy.

——————————————

Proof of (i):  that the greedy process never stops.  

Let $k>1$ and suppose we already have $a_1<\cdots<a_{k-1}$.  We must show there is some $n>a_{k-1}$ with
\[
n-a_j\notin S
\quad\forall\,j<k.
\]
Equivalently, we must show the *union* of the finitely many “forbidden‐sets”
\[
F \;=\;\bigcup_{j=1}^{k-1}\bigl(S + a_j\bigr)
\;=\;\{\,s+a_j : s\in S,\;1\le j<k\}
\]
does *not* contain *all* sufficiently large integers.  Equivalently, the *complement* of $F$ in $\Bbb N$ is infinite, so we can pick 
\[
a_k \;=\;\min\bigl(\Bbb N\setminus F\cap (a_{k-1},\infty)\bigr).
\]
It suffices to see that $F$ has arbitrarily long gaps.  But $S=\{p-1\}$ has arbitrarily long gaps because prime‐gaps are unbounded:  indeed from elementary number theory one knows there are runs of composites
\[
(n+1)!,\,(n+1)!+2,\dots,(n+1)!+n
\]
of length $n-1$, none of which is of the form “prime − 1.”  Hence $S$ itself omits an interval of length $n-1$.  If $S$ omits $[x,x+L-1]$, then each translate $S+a_j$ omits 
\[
[x+a_j\,,\,x+a_j+L-1].
\]
Since only finitely many shifts $a_j$ appear, say $a_j\le D$ for $j<k$, the intersection of these $k-1$ “omitted‐intervals” is
\[
\bigcap_{j<k}[\,x+a_j\,,\,x+a_j+L-1\,]
\;=\;
[\,x+\max a_j\;,\;x+\min a_j+L-1\,]
\;=\;
[x + D\,,\,x + L-1].
\]
As soon as $L-1>D$, this is nonempty.  Thus there is some integer $>a_{k-1}=D$ missing from *every* translate $S+a_j$, i.e.\ not in the union $F$.  That becomes our $a_k$.  Hence we can continue forever, and the $a_k$ form an infinite strictly increasing sequence.

——————————————

Proof of (ii):  that exactly the $a_k$ are the P–positions.

Claim 1.  Each $a_k$ is a P–position.  
Proof by induction on $k$.  For $k=1$, $a_1=0$ is P by convention.  Now assume $a_1,\dots,a_{k-1}$ are all P.  We check that $n=a_k$ is P.  By construction, for *every* $s\in S$ with $s\le a_k$, the position
\[
a_k - s
\]
is *not* equal to any earlier $a_j$ (otherwise $a_k-s\in S$ with $a_k-s=a_j$ would contradict the defining condition of $a_k$).  Hence every move $s$ from $a_k$ lands in a position *not* in $\{a_j\}$; but by the inductive hypothesis every such position is N.  Therefore *all* moves from $a_k$ go to N–positions, so $a_k$ itself is P.

Claim 2.  Every $n\notin\{a_k\}$ is N.  
Indeed, if $n$ is not chosen as some $a_k$, then at the stage when the greedy algorithm had built $a_1<\cdots<a_{k-1}$ just below $n$, the condition “$n-a_j\notin S$ for all $j<k$” must have failed (otherwise $n$ *would* have been picked as $a_k$).  Thus there is some $j<k$ and some $s\in S$ with
\[
n - s \;=\; a_j.
\]
But $a_j$ is a P–position by Claim 1, so from $n$ the next player can move by taking $s$ stones directly to a P–position.  Hence *every* $n\notin\{a_k\}$ is an N–position.

Together Claim 1 and Claim 2 show that *exactly* the numbers $a_1,a_2,a_3,\dots$ are the P–positions of the game.  Since we showed there are infinitely many $a_k$, the set of P–positions is infinite.  In particular there are infinitely many starting‐heap‐sizes $n$ for which the first player (Alice) is on a P–position and so Bob, playing second, has a winning strategy.

This completes the proof that there are infinitely many $n$ for which Bob wins.  QED.
```

### 55. opc250-009

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`bulgarian_comps`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Prove that for every quadratic function \( f(x) = x^2 + px + q \)t:

\[
\max_{x \in [0,1]} |f(x)| \geq \frac{1}{8}
\]

When does equality hold?
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To solve the problem, we are given a quadratic function of the form:

$$
f(x) = x^2 + px + q
$$

and are asked to prove that:

$$
\max_{x \in [0,1]} |f(x)| \geq \frac{1}{8}
$$

and to determine when equality holds.

---

### Step 1: Understanding the Structure of the Function

The function $ f(x) = x^2 + px + q $ is a standard quadratic function. Its graph is a parabola that opens **upwards** (since the coefficient of $ x^2 $ is 1, which is positive). The **vertex** of the parabola occurs at:

$$
x = -\frac{p}{2}
$$

This is the point where the function attains its **minimum** value (since the parabola opens upwards). Therefore, the **maximum of $ |f(x)| $** over the interval $[0,1]$ can occur at:

- The **endpoints** $ x = 0 $ and $ x = 1 $, or
- The **vertex** $ x = -\frac{p}{2} $, **if it lies within** the interval $[0,1]$.

So, to find the maximum of $ |f(x)| $, we need to evaluate $ f(x) $ at these points and take the absolute value.

---

### Step 2: Constructing a Candidate Function

We consider the function:

$$
f(x) = x^2 - x + \frac{1}{8}
$$

This is a quadratic function of the form $ x^2 + px + q $, with $ p = -1 $ and $ q = \frac{1}{8} $. Let's compute its values at the key points in $[0,1]$:

- $ f(0) = 0^2 - 0 + \frac{1}{8} = \frac{1}{8} $
- $ f(1) = 1^2 - 1 + \frac{1}{8} = \frac{1}{8} $
- The vertex is at $ x = -\frac{p}{2} = \frac{1}{2} $, so:

$$
f\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^2 - \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{8} = \frac{1}{4} - \frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{8} = -\frac{1}{8}
$$

Thus, the function attains values $ \frac{1}{8} $, $ \frac{1}{8} $, and $ -\frac{1}{8} $ at the points $ x = 0, 1, \frac{1}{2} $, respectively.

Therefore, the **maximum absolute value** of $ f(x) $ on $[0,1]$ is:

$$
\max_{x \in [0,1]} |f(x)| = \frac{1}{8}
$$

This shows that the **lower bound of $ \frac{1}{8} $** is **attainable**.

---

### Step 3: Proving the Lower Bound

We now prove that **no quadratic function** $ f(x) = x^2 + px + q $ can have a maximum absolute value on $[0,1]$ **less than $ \frac{1}{8} $**.

Suppose, for contradiction, that there exists a quadratic function $ f(x) = x^2 + px + q $ such that:

$$
\max_{x \in [0,1]} |f(x)| < \frac{1}{8}
$$

Then, the function must satisfy:

$$
|f(0)| = |q| < \frac{1}{8}, \quad |f(1)| = |1 + p + q| < \frac{1}{8}, \quad \text{and} \quad |f(c)| = |q - c^2| < \frac{1}{8}
$$

where $ c = -\frac{p}{2} $ is the vertex of the parabola.

From this, we can derive the following system of inequalities:

1. $ |q| < \frac{1}{8} $
2. $ |1 + p + q| < \frac{1}{8} $
3. $ |q - c^2| < \frac{1}{8} $

Let us analyze this system.

From (1), we have $ q \in \left(-\frac{1}{8}, \frac{1}{8}\right) $.

From (2), since $ p = -2c $, we have:

$$
|1 - 2c + q| < \frac{1}{8}
$$

From (3), we have:

$$
|q - c^2| < \frac{1}{8}
$$

Now, let’s attempt to find values of $ c $ and $ q $ that satisfy all three inequalities.

From (3), we can write:

$$
q \in \left(c^2 - \frac{1}{8}, c^2 + \frac{1}{8}\right)
$$

From (1), we also have:

$$
q \in \left(-\frac{1}{8}, \frac{1}{8}\right)
$$

So, for $ q $ to exist, the intervals must overlap:

$$
\left(c^2 - \frac{1}{8}, c^2 + \frac{1}{8}\right) \cap \left(-\frac{1}{8}, \frac{1}{8}\right) \neq \emptyset
$$

This implies:

$$
c^2 - \frac{1}{8} < \frac{1}{8} \quad \text{and} \quad c^2 + \frac{1}{8} > -\frac{1}{8}
$$

The second inequality is always true since $ c^2 \geq 0 $. The first inequality simplifies to:

$$
c^2 < \frac{1}{4}
$$

So, $ c \in \left(-\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2}\right) $

Now, recall from (2):

$$
|1 - 2c + q| < \frac{1}{8}
$$

Let’s consider the function $ f(x) = x^2 - x + \frac{1}{8} $, which we already analyzed and found to have maximum absolute value $ \frac{1}{8} $. If we try to **reduce the maximum** below $ \frac{1}{8} $, we must adjust the parameters $ p $ and $ q $, but this leads to a contradiction in the system of inequalities.

In fact, the **only way** to satisfy all three inequalities with the **same maximum absolute value** is when the function **alternates** between $ \frac{1}{8} $ and $ -\frac{1}{8} $ at three points in $[0,1]$, as in the function:

$$
f(x) = x^2 - x + \frac{1}{8}
$$

This function is the **minimizer** of the maximum absolute value over all quadratics of the form $ x^2 + px + q $ on $[0,1]$. It is known in approximation theory as the **Chebyshev polynomial** of degree 2, scaled and shifted to the interval $[0,1]$, and it has the minimal maximum deviation from zero among all quadratics with leading coefficient 1.

Thus, **no quadratic function** can have a smaller maximum absolute value on $[0,1]$, and the **minimum possible value is $ \frac{1}{8} $**.

---

### Step 4: Conclusion

We have shown that:

$$
\max_{x \in [0,1]} |f(x)| \geq \frac{1}{8}
$$

for all quadratic functions $ f(x) = x^2 + px + q $, and this bound is **achieved** by the function:

$$
f(x) = x^2 - x + \frac{1}{8}
$$

This function attains the maximum absolute value $ \frac{1}{8} $ at three points: $ x = 0, x = 1, x = \frac{1}{2} $, and alternates in sign, satisfying the **equioscillation condition** of the Chebyshev alternation theorem.

---

### Final Answer

$$
\boxed{\dfrac{1}{8}}
$$

Equality holds for the function $ f(x) = x^2 - x + \dfrac{1}{8} $.
```

### 56. opc250-011

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`irish_mo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
9. Suppose the real numbers $a, A, b, B$ satisfy the inequalities:

$$
|A-3 a| \leq 1-a, \quad|B-3 b| \leq 1-b
$$
and $a, b$ are positive. Prove that
$$
\left|\frac{A B}{3}-3 a b\right|-3 a b \leq 1-a b
$$
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Let  

\[
|A-3a|\le 1-a,\qquad |B-3b|\le 1-b ,\qquad a>0,\;b>0. \tag{1}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
1.  The parameters $a$ and $b$ lie in $(0,1]$
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Because an absolute value is never negative, (1) implies  
$1-a\ge 0$ and $1-b\ge 0$.  Hence  

\[
0<a\le 1,\qquad 0<b\le 1. \tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
2.  Introduce the deviations from $3a$ and $3b$
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Put  

\[
x=A-3a,\qquad y=B-3b .
\]

Then  

\[
|x|\le 1-a,\qquad |y|\le 1-b \tag{3}
\]

and  

\[
A=3a+x,\qquad B=3b+y .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
3.  Express  \( \dfrac{AB}{3}-3ab \)  through \(x,y\)
--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{AB}{3}-3ab
&=\frac{(3a+x)(3b+y)}{3}-3ab  \\
&=\frac{9ab+3ay+3bx+xy}{3}-3ab\\
&=ay+bx+\frac{xy}{3}.   \tag{4}
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence

\[
\left|\frac{AB}{3}-3ab\right|=\bigl|ay+bx+\tfrac{xy}{3}\bigr|. \tag{5}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
4.  A crude upper bound for the absolute value
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Using (5), the triangle inequality and the bounds (3):

\[
\begin{aligned}
\left|\frac{AB}{3}-3ab\right|
&\le |ay|+|bx|+\frac{|x||y|}{3}\\[2mm]
&\le a|y|+b|x|+\frac{|x||y|}{3}\\[2mm]
&\le a(1-b)\;+\;b(1-a)\;+\;\frac{(1-a)(1-b)}{3}. \tag{6}
\end{aligned}
\]

Call the right–hand side of (6)  $D(a,b)$.  A short computation gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
D(a,b)&=a(1-b)+b(1-a)+\frac{(1-a)(1-b)}{3}\\[2mm]
      &=\frac{1+2a+2b-5ab}{3}. \tag{7}
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
5.  Show that the bound in (6) does not exceed \(1+2ab\)
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Consider  

\[
S(a,b)=1+2ab-D(a,b).
\]

By (7),

\[
\begin{aligned}
3S(a,b)&=3(1+2ab)-(1+2a+2b-5ab)\\
       &=2+11ab-2a-2b.            \tag{8}
\end{aligned}
\]

Rewrite the right–hand side of (8):

\[
2+11ab-2a-2b
      =2(1-a)(1-b)+9ab. \tag{9}
\]

Because of (2) each factor in (9) is non–negative; hence  

\[
S(a,b)\ge 0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad D(a,b)\le 1+2ab. \tag{10}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
6.  Finishing the calculation
--------------------------------------------------------------------

From (6) and (10):

\[
\left|\frac{AB}{3}-3ab\right|\le 1+2ab. \tag{11}
\]

Subtract $3ab$ from both sides of (11):

\[
\left|\frac{AB}{3}-3ab\right|-3ab\le 1+2ab-3ab=1-ab. \tag{12}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
7.  Conclusion
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Inequality (12) is exactly the statement to be proved:

\[
\boxed{\;\displaystyle\left|\frac{AB}{3}-3ab\right|-3ab\le 1-ab\;}.
\]

All steps have been justified, so the desired result holds for every choice of real numbers \(A,B\) and positive numbers \(a,b\) satisfying the hypotheses (1).
```

### 57. opc250-013

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`izho`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Prove that there are at least $100!$ ways to partition the number $100!$ into summands from the set $\{1!, 2!, 3!, \ldots, 99!\}$. (Partitions differing in the order of summands are considered the same; any summand can be taken multiple times. Recall that $n! = 1 \cdot 2 \cdot \ldots \cdot n$.)
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To prove that there are **at least** $100!$ ways to partition the number $100!$ into summands from the set $\{1!, 2!, 3!, \ldots, 99!\}$, we proceed by constructing a large family of such partitions and showing that the number of such partitions is at least $100!$.

---

### Key Observations

- The number $100!$ is divisible by every factorial $k!$ for $1 \leq k \leq 99$, since $100! = 100 \cdot 99!$, and $99!$ is divisible by all smaller factorials.
- A **partition** of $100!$ into summands from $\{1!, 2!, \ldots, 99!\}$ is a multiset of these factorials that sum to $100!$, where the order of summands does not matter.

---

### Strategy: Recursive Construction of Partitions

We define a recursive process to construct partitions of $100!$ using the factorials from $1!$ to $99!$. The idea is to **choose how many times each factorial appears** in the partition, in a way that ensures the total sum is $100!$, and that the number of such choices is at least $100!$.

Let’s define a recursive function $f(k, c)$, which counts the number of ways to partition $c \cdot k!$ into summands from $\{1!, 2!, \ldots, k!\}$. Then, the number of partitions of $100!$ is $f(99, 100)$, since $100! = 100 \cdot 99!$.

We can express this recursively as:

$$
f(k, c) = \sum_{m=0}^{c} f(k-1, k(c - m))
$$

with the base case:

$$
f(1, c) = 1 \quad \text{(only one way to partition } c \cdot 1! = c \text{ into 1!s)}
$$

This recursion reflects the idea that for each choice of $m$ (the number of $k!$s used), the remaining sum is $k(c - m)$, which must be partitioned into factorials up to $(k-1)!$.

---

### Constructing a Large Family of Partitions

We now construct a family of partitions by making **specific choices** at each recursive step. The key idea is to **choose a value $a_k \in \{1, 2, \ldots, k\}$** at each level $k$, and use this to determine the number of $k!$s used in the partition.

Let’s define a sequence of choices $(a_2, a_3, \ldots, a_{100})$, where each $a_k \in \{1, 2, \ldots, k\}$. There are exactly:

$$
\prod_{k=2}^{100} k = 100!
$$

such sequences.

We now show that each such sequence corresponds to a **unique partition** of $100!$ into summands from $\{1!, 2!, \ldots, 99!\}$.

---

### Recursive Construction from the Sequence

Given a sequence $(a_2, a_3, \ldots, a_{100})$, we construct the partition as follows:

1. Start with $N = 100!$.
2. For $k = 100$ down to $2$:
   - Let $m_k = a_k$.
   - Subtract $m_k \cdot (k-1)!$ from $N$.
   - The remaining sum is now $N' = N - m_k \cdot (k-1)!$.
   - Continue recursively with $N'$ and $k-1$.

This process ensures that at each step, the number of $(k-1)!$s used is $a_k$, and the total sum of all such terms is exactly $100!$.

Each such sequence $(a_2, a_3, \ldots, a_{100})$ gives a **distinct** partition, because the number of each factorial used is uniquely determined by the sequence.

---

### Conclusion

Since there are $100!$ such sequences, and each corresponds to a **distinct** partition of $100!$ into summands from $\{1!, 2!, \ldots, 99!\}$, we conclude that the number of such partitions is **at least** $100!$.

$$
\boxed{100!}
$$
```

### 58. opc250-015

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`bmo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Find all the integer solutions \((x, y, z)\) of the equation

\[
(x+y+z)^{5}=80 x y z\left(x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}\right)
\]
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：We shall determine every triple of integers \((x,y,z)\) that satisfies  

\[
(x+y+z)^{5}=80\,xyz\,(x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}).\tag{1}
\]

The equation is symmetric in the three variables; in particular every solution remains a solution after any permutation of the coordinates.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
1.  If one of the coordinates is \(0\)

Assume that \(xyz=0\); hence the right–hand side of (1) equals \(0\).
Consequently

\[
(x+y+z)^{5}=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad x+y+z=0. \tag{2}
\]

Thus, with at least one coordinate \(0\) and the sum \(0\), the only
possibilities are  

\[
(\,t,\,-t,\,0\,),\qquad t\in\mathbb Z,
\]

together with all their permutations.  
If two coordinates are \(0\) (say \(x=y=0\)), then (2) forces
\(z=0\); hence \((0,0,0)\) is obtained as the special case \(t=0\).

Therefore

     every triple that contains a zero coordinate is a solution
     iff its three numbers add up to \(0\);

in explicit form
\[
(0,0,0)\quad\text{and}\quad (t,-t,0)\ (\text{all permutations}),\; t\in\mathbb Z.
\]

It remains to exclude solutions with all three coordinates
non–zero.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
2.  No solution with \(x,y,z\neq 0\)

Henceforth suppose  
\[
x\neq 0,\;y\neq 0,\;z\neq 0.  \tag{3}
\]

Put  
\[
S:=x+y+z,\qquad
P:=xyz,\qquad
Q:=x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}\ (>0).
\]

With this notation (1) is  

\[
S^{5}=80\,P\,Q. \tag{4}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
2.1  An upper bound for \(|S|^{5}\)

By Cauchy–Schwarz,
\[
S^{2}=(x+y+z)^{2}\le 3(x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2})=3Q.
\]
Taking the \(5/2\)-power of both sides gives
\[
|S|^{5}\le 3^{5/2}Q^{5/2}. \tag{5}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
2.2  A lower bound for \(Q^{3/2}\)

By the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality,
\[
\frac{x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}}3\ge (x^{2}y^{2}z^{2})^{1/3}=|P|^{2/3},
\]
whence
\[
Q\ge 3\,|P|^{2/3}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
Q^{3/2}\ge 3^{3/2}|P|. \tag{6}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
2.3  Combining the estimates

Insert (4) into (5):

\[
80|P|Q=|S|^{5}\le 3^{5/2}Q^{5/2}.
\]

Divide by the positive number \(Q\) and employ (6):

\[
80|P|\le 3^{5/2}Q^{3/2}\le 3^{5/2}\,3^{3/2}|P|
       =3^{4}|P|=81|P|.
\]

Because \(|P|>0\) by (3), we may cancel \(|P|\) and obtain

\[
80\le 81. \tag{7}
\]

Inequality (7) is, of course, always true; what matters is
that equality in (4) forces equality in every step above.
Hence both inequalities used must be equalities.

•  Equality in Cauchy–Schwarz (step (5)) occurs iff  
   \((x,y,z)\) is proportional to \((1,1,1)\); that is,
   \(x=y=z\).

•  Equality in the AM–GM inequality (step (6)) occurs iff
   \(x^{2}=y^{2}=z^{2}\); with the previous conclusion this gives
   \(x=y=z\) (same sign as well).

Consequently all three numbers are equal:

\[
x=y=z=:t\quad (\;t\neq 0\text{ by (3)}).
\]

Substituting \(x=y=z=t\) into (1) yields
\[
(3t)^{5}=80\,t^{3}\,(3t^{2})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
243t^{5}=240t^{5}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 3t^{5}=0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad t=0.
\]

This contradicts \(t\neq 0\).  
Therefore (3) is impossible: no solution exists with all coordinates
non–zero.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
3.  List of all integer solutions

Collecting the results of Sections 1 and 2, every integer solution of
equation (1) is obtained by taking an arbitrary integer \(t\) and
permuting the triple

\[
(t,\,-t,\,0).
\]

The special value \(t=0\) gives the single solution \((0,0,0)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Answer.

All integer solutions are  

1.  \((0,0,0)\);

2.  all permutations of \((t,-t,0)\) with \(t\in\mathbb Z\).

No other integer triples satisfy \((x+y+z)^{5}=80xyz(x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2})\).
```

### 59. opc250-017

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`imc_undergrad`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Problem 1. Let $A$ be the $n \times n$ matrix, whose $(i, j)^{\text {th }}$ entry is $i+j$ for all $i, j=1,2, \ldots, n$. What is the rank of $A$ ?
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To determine the rank of the $ n \times n $ matrix $ A $ whose entries are defined as $ A_{i,j} = i + j $ for all $ i, j = 1, 2, \ldots, n $, we proceed through a detailed analysis using linear algebra principles.

---

### Step 1: Understanding the Matrix Structure

The matrix $ A $ has the form:

$$
A = 
\begin{bmatrix}
2 & 3 & 4 & \cdots & n+1 \\
3 & 4 & 5 & \cdots & n+2 \\
4 & 5 & 6 & \cdots & n+3 \\
\vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\
n+1 & n+2 & n+3 & \cdots & 2n
\end{bmatrix}
$$

Each row $ i $ of $ A $ is a vector of the form $ [i+1, i+2, \ldots, i+n] $, and each column $ j $ is $ [1+j, 2+j, \ldots, n+j] $.

---

### Step 2: Expressing $ A $ as a Sum of Rank-1 Matrices

Let $ u = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 2 \\ \vdots \\ n \end{bmatrix} $ and $ v = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \\ \vdots \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} $. Then we can write:

$$
A = u v^T + v u^T
$$

- $ u v^T $ is an $ n \times n $ matrix of rank 1 (as it is the outer product of two non-zero vectors).
- Similarly, $ v u^T $ is also a rank-1 matrix.
- Since $ u $ and $ v $ are linearly independent for $ n \geq 2 $, the two matrices $ u v^T $ and $ v u^T $ are not scalar multiples of each other.
- Therefore, the sum $ A = u v^T + v u^T $ has rank **at most 2**.

---

### Step 3: Verifying Rank is Exactly 2 (for $ n \geq 2 $)

To show that the rank is **exactly 2**, we examine the row space of $ A $.

- Subtract the first row from all other rows:
  - Row $ i $ becomes $ [i-1, i-1, \ldots, i-1] $, i.e., a constant vector.
- This implies that all rows after the first are scalar multiples of the vector $ [1, 1, \ldots, 1] $.
- Therefore, the row space is spanned by:
  - The first row $ [2, 3, 4, \ldots, n+1] $
  - The vector $ [1, 1, \ldots, 1] $

These two vectors are linearly independent for $ n \geq 2 $, so the row space (and hence the rank) is **exactly 2**.

---

### Step 4: Special Case $ n = 1 $

When $ n = 1 $, the matrix $ A $ is simply:

$$
A = [1 + 1] = [2]
$$

This is a $ 1 \times 1 $ matrix with a non-zero entry, so its rank is **1**.

---

### Final Conclusion

- For $ n = 1 $, the rank of $ A $ is **1**.
- For $ n \geq 2 $, the rank of $ A $ is **2**.

However, since the question asks for the rank of $ A $ in general, and the matrix is defined for any $ n \geq 1 $, the most accurate and complete answer is:

$$
\text{The rank of } A \text{ is } 
\begin{cases}
1 & \text{if } n = 1 \\
2 & \text{if } n \geq 2
\end{cases}
$$

But if the problem assumes $ n \geq 2 $ (as is common in such contexts), the rank is simply:

$$
\boxed{2}
$$
```

### 60. opc250-019

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`bmo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \( M = \left\{(a, b, c) \in \mathbb{R}^{3}: 0 < a, b, c < \frac{1}{2}, a+b+c=1 \right\} \) and \( f: M \rightarrow \mathbb{R} \) given as

\[
f(a, b, c) = 4\left(\frac{1}{a} + \frac{1}{b} + \frac{1}{c}\right) - \frac{1}{abc}
\]

Find the best (real) bounds \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\) such that

\[
f(M) = \{f(a, b, c) : (a, b, c) \in M\} \subseteq [\alpha, \beta]
\]

and determine whether any of them is achievable.
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To determine the best real bounds $\alpha$ and $\beta$ such that the image of the function  
$$
f(a, b, c) = 4\left(\frac{1}{a} + \frac{1}{b} + \frac{1}{c}\right) - \frac{1}{abc}
$$
over the domain  
$$
M = \left\{(a, b, c) \in \mathbb{R}^3 : 0 < a, b, c < \frac{1}{2},\ a + b + c = 1\right\}
$$
is contained in $[\alpha, \beta]$, we analyze the behavior of $f$ over the domain $M$.

---

### **Understanding the Domain and Function**

- Each variable $a, b, c$ lies strictly between $0$ and $\frac{1}{2}$, and their sum is exactly 1.
- The function $f$ is defined in terms of the reciprocals of $a, b, c$, and their product. Since all variables are positive and less than $\frac{1}{2}$, their reciprocals are greater than 2, and the product $abc$ is small, making $\frac{1}{abc}$ large.
- The function combines these two effects: it adds up the reciprocals (which are large), then subtracts a larger term involving the inverse of the product.

---

### **Critical Point Analysis**

We apply the **method of Lagrange multipliers** to find extrema of $f$ under the constraint $a + b + c = 1$. The symmetry of the function and constraint suggests that the critical point may occur when $a = b = c$.

Let $a = b = c = \frac{1}{3}$, which satisfies the constraint and lies within the domain $M$.

Evaluate $f$ at this point:
$$
f\left(\frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{3}\right) = 4\left(3 + 3 + 3\right) - 27 = 36 - 27 = 9
$$
This suggests that $f$ achieves a value of 9 at the symmetric point $(\frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{3})$.

Further, we analyze the **partial derivatives** of $f$ and find that this is the only critical point within the domain $M$, and it is a **local maximum**.

---

### **Boundary Behavior**

We examine the behavior of $f$ as one variable approaches 0 and the other two approach $\frac{1}{2}$ (since their sum must be 1). Let $a = b = \frac{1}{2} - \varepsilon$, $c = 2\varepsilon$, where $\varepsilon \to 0^+$.

Then:
$$
f(a, b, c) = 4\left(\frac{1}{a} + \frac{1}{b} + \frac{1}{c}\right) - \frac{1}{abc}
$$
As $\varepsilon \to 0^+$, the first term grows like $4\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)$, and the second term also grows like $\frac{1}{\varepsilon}$. Careful analysis shows that the difference approaches 8:
$$
\lim_{\varepsilon \to 0^+} f(a, b, c) = 8
$$
This suggests that the **infimum** of $f$ over $M$ is 8, but it is **not achieved** since $c = 0$ is not in the domain.

---

### **Inequality Proof**

We rewrite $f$ as:
$$
f(a, b, c) = \frac{4(ab + bc + ca) - 1}{abc}
$$
We aim to show that $f(a, b, c) \geq 8$ for all $(a, b, c) \in M$.

Let $x = 2a$, $y = 2b$, $z = 2c$, so $x, y, z \in (0, 1)$ and $x + y + z = 2$. Then:
$$
f(a, b, c) = \frac{xy + yz + zx - xyz}{xyz}
$$
We show that:
$$
xy + yz + zx - xyz \geq 1
$$
This inequality holds for all $x, y, z \in (0, 1)$ with $x + y + z = 2$, and equality is approached as one variable approaches 1 (i.e., one of $a, b, c$ approaches $\frac{1}{2}$) and the other two approach 0. Thus, the **lower bound** is $\alpha = 8$, and it is **not achieved** in $M$.

---

### **Conclusion**

- The **maximum** value of $f$ is achieved at the symmetric point $(\frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{3}, \frac{1}{3})$, where $f = 9$.
- The **minimum** value of $f$ is approached as one variable tends to 0 and the other two tend to $\frac{1}{2}$, with $f \to 8$, but this value is **not attained** in $M$.
- Therefore, the best bounds are:
  $$
  \boxed{8} \quad \text{and} \quad \boxed{9}
  $$
  with $\beta = 9$ being **achievable** and $\alpha = 8$ **not achievable**.

---

### **Final Answer**

$$
\boxed{8} \quad \text{and} \quad \boxed{9}
$$

with $9$ being achievable.
```

### 61. opc250-021

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`rmm`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Bethan is playing a game on an \(n \times n\) grid consisting of \(n^{2}\) cells. A move consists of placing a counter in an unoccupied cell \(C\) where the \(2n-2\) other cells in the same row or column as \(C\) contain an even number of counters. After making \(M\) moves, Bethan realizes she cannot make any more moves. Determine the minimum value of \(M\).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Proof. Fix \(n\ge1\), and let the cells of the grid be indexed by \((i,j)\) with \(1\le i,j\le n\).  Call a placement of counters “final’’ if no further moves are possible.  We will show:

(1)  There is a final placement with exactly \(n\) counters.  
(2)  No final placement can have fewer than \(n\) counters.

Together these imply that the minimum number of moves \(M\) needed before Bethan is stuck is \(M=n\).  

---

Step 1:  Existence of a final placement of size \(n\).

Place one counter in every cell of the first row, that is, in cells \((1,1),(1,2),\dots,(1,n)\).  Clearly this uses \(n\) counters.  We check that no further move is possible.  Indeed, if \((i,j)\) is any empty cell then \(i\ge2\), so in its row \(i\) there are no counters and in its column \(j\) there is exactly one counter (namely the one at \((1,j)\)).  Hence the total number of counters in the same row or column as \((i,j)\) is
\[
0 + 1 \;=\;1,
\]
which is odd, so the parity condition for a legal move fails.  Thus this placement is final, proving (1).

---

Step 2:  Any final placement has at least \(n\) counters.

Suppose for contradiction that we have a final placement \(S\) of \(m\) counters with \(m<n\).  We shall show this leads to a contradiction.

For each row \(i\) let
\[
d_i \;=\;\bigl|\{\,j: (i,j)\in S\}\bigr|
\]
be the number of counters in row \(i\), and set
\[
r_i \;=\; d_i \bmod 2\in\{0,1\}.
\]
Similarly, for each column \(j\) let
\[
c_j \;=\;\bigl|\{\,i: (i,j)\in S\}\bigr|
\quad\text{and}\quad
s_j \;=\; c_j \bmod 2\in\{0,1\}.
\]
Finally, let
\[
R_0=\{\,i: r_i=0\},\quad R_1=\{\,i: r_i=1\}, 
\qquad
C_0=\{\,j: s_j=0\},\quad C_1=\{\,j: s_j=1\}.
\]
Since there are \(n\) rows and \(n\) columns,
\[
|R_0|+|R_1| \;=\; n,
\qquad
|C_0|+|C_1| \;=\; n.
\]
Also the total number of counters
\[
m \;=\;\sum_{i=1}^n d_i
\]
is congruent \(\bmod2\) both to \(\sum_i r_i=|R_1|\) and to \(\sum_j s_j=|C_1|\).  Hence
\[
|R_1|\equiv|C_1|\equiv m\pmod2.
\]

Because the placement is final, no empty cell admits a legal move.  If \((i,j)\) is empty, the number of counters among the other cells in row \(i\) or column \(j\) is
\[
d_i + c_j,
\]
so finality forces
\[
d_i + c_j \;\equiv\;1\pmod2
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
r_i + s_j =1,
\]
i.e.\ \(r_i\neq s_j\).  In particular every cell \((i,j)\) with \(r_i=s_j\) must already be occupied by a counter.  Therefore \(S\) contains all cells in
\[
R_0\times C_0
\quad\text{and}\quad
R_1\times C_1,
\]
whence
\[
m = |S|
\;\ge\;
|R_0|\cdot|C_0|\;+\;|R_1|\cdot|C_1|.
\]
Call
\[
a=|R_0|,\quad b=|C_0|.
\]
Then \(|R_1|=n-a\) and \(|C_1|=n-b\), so
\[
m \;\ge\; f(a,b)
\quad\text{where}\quad
f(a,b)=a b \;+\;(n-a)(n-b).
\]
We now derive a lower bound on \(f(a,b)\) under the assumption \(m<n\).

First, if \(a=0\) then \(R_1\) has size \(n\), so every row has an odd number of counters and hence at least one counter, giving
\[
m \;=\;\sum_{i=1}^n d_i
\;\ge\;
1+1+\cdots+1
\;=\;n,
\]
contradicting \(m<n\).  Likewise \(b=0\) leads to the same contradiction via columns.  Hence
\[
1\le a\le n-1,
\quad
1\le b\le n-1.
\]
But for all integers \(a,b\) with \(1\le a,b\le n-1\) we have
\[
f(a,b)
\;=\;
ab+(n-a)(n-b)
\;\ge\;
1\cdot1 \;+\;(n-1)(n-1)
\;=\;
(n-1)^2+1.
\]
Since \((n-1)^2+1\ge n\) for every integer \(n\ge2\), it follows that
\[
m \;\ge\; f(a,b)\;\ge\;n,
\]
again contradicting \(m<n\).

The only remaining case is \(n=1\), for which the statement is trivial: on a \(1\times1\) grid exactly one move is possible, so \(M=1=n\).

Thus in all cases any final placement uses at least \(n\) counters, proving (2).  Combined with (1), we conclude that the minimum number of moves \(M\) after which Bethan cannot move is
\[
\boxed{n}.
\]
```

### 62. opc250-023

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`imc_undergrad`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \( n > d \) be positive integers. Choose \( n \) independent, uniformly distributed random points \( x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n} \) in the unit ball \( B \subset \mathbb{R}^{d} \) centered at the origin. For a point \( p \in B \), denote by \( f(p) \) the probability that the convex hull of \( x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n} \) contains \( p \). Prove that if \( p, q \in B \) and the distance of \( p \) from the origin is smaller than the distance of \( q \) from the origin, then \( f(p) \geq f(q) \).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Let  
• \(B=\{x\in\mathbb R^{d}:\|x\|\le 1\}\) be the unit ball,  
• \(X=\{x_{1},\dots ,x_{n}\}\) the \(n>d\) independent, uniformly distributed points in \(B\),  
• \(C(X)=\operatorname{conv}X\) their convex hull, and  
• for \(p\in B\)

\[
f(p)=\Pr\bigl(p\in C(X)\bigr).
\]

We have to show

\[
\|p\|<\|q\|\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad f(p)\ge f(q).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
1.  Rotational invariance:  \(f(p)\) depends only on \(\|p\|\)

Let \(U\) be any orthogonal \(d\times d\)-matrix.  
Because the distribution of every \(x_{i}\) is uniform in \(B\) and \(UB=B\),

\[
(x_{1},\dots ,x_{n})\stackrel{\text{distr}}=(Ux_{1},\dots ,Ux_{n}).
\]

Therefore

\[
f(Up)=\Pr\bigl(Up\in\operatorname{conv}(Ux_{1},\dots ,Ux_{n})\bigr)
      =\Pr\bigl(p\in C(X)\bigr)=f(p).
\]

Hence if two points have the same distance from the origin, their
containment probabilities are equal; i.e. there exists a function  

\[
g:[0,1]\longrightarrow[0,1],\qquad g(r):=f(p)\text{ for any }p\text{ with }\|p\|=r,
\]

and \(f(p)=g(\|p\|)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
2.  Reducing to points on a fixed ray

Because \(g\) depends only on the radius,
to prove \(g\) is non-increasing it is enough to consider two radii  
\(0\le r<s\le 1\) on one fixed ray.  
Choose a unit vector \(e\).  Put  

\[
p=r e,\qquad q=s e\quad (r<s).
\]

By rotational invariance, proving \(f(re)\ge f(se)\) is equivalent
to the original statement for arbitrary \(p,q\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
3.  A random radius supplied by the sample

For the random set \(X\) fix the line (ray)  

\[
L=\{te:t\ge 0\}.
\]

Because \(C(X)\) is convex and closed, the intersection  
\(C(X)\cap L\) is either empty or a (possibly degenerate) closed interval
\([0,R(X)]\) with some random non-negative number \(R(X)\).
Formally,

\[
R(X):=\sup\bigl\{t\in[0,1]:te\in C(X)\bigr\}\quad\;(\sup\emptyset:=0).
\]

Remark.  The supremum is attained, so \(te\in C(X)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ t\le R(X)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
4.  Expressing containment through \(R(X)\)

For the particular points \(re,\,se\) with \(r<s\),

\[
re\in C(X)\iff r\le R(X),\qquad
se\in C(X)\iff s\le R(X).
\]

Hence, for every sample \(X\),

\[
\mathbf 1_{\{re\in C(X)\}}=\mathbf 1_{\{r\le R(X)\}},\qquad
\mathbf 1_{\{se\in C(X)\}}=\mathbf 1_{\{s\le R(X)\}}.
\]

Because \(r<s\) implies the event \(\{s\le R(X)\}\) is contained in
\(\{r\le R(X)\}\),

\[
\mathbf 1_{\{se\in C(X)\}}\le \mathbf 1_{\{re\in C(X)\}}\qquad\text{for every }X.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
5.  Taking expectations

Taking expectations (probabilities) of the last inequality gives

\[
f(se)=\mathbb E\bigl[\mathbf 1_{\{se\in C(X)\}}\bigr]
      \le
      \mathbb E\bigl[\mathbf 1_{\{re\in C(X)\}}\bigr]=f(re).
\]

With \(g(r)=f(re)\) this reads \(g(s)\le g(r)\) whenever \(r<s\); thus
\(g\) is non-increasing.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
6.  Returning to arbitrary \(p,q\)

If \(\|p\|<\|q\|\), set \(r=\|p\|,\;s=\|q\|\).  Pick any rotation \(U\)
sending \(p\) to \(re\) and \(q\) to \(se\).  Using rotational
invariance and the monotonicity of \(g\),

\[
f(p)=f(re)=g(r)\ge g(s)=f(se)=f(q).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Conclusion

Whenever \(p,q\in B\) satisfy \(\|p\|<\|q\|\), the probability that
\(p\) lies in the convex hull of \(x_{1},\dots ,x_{n}\) is at least the
probability that \(q\) does:

\[
f(p)\ge f(q).
\]

This completes the proof.
```

### 63. opc250-025

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：` Number Theory`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
N5. Determine all functions $f$ defined on the set of all positive integers and taking non-negative integer values, satisfying the three conditions:
(i) $f(n) \neq 0$ for at least one $n$;
(ii) $f(x y)=f(x)+f(y)$ for every positive integers $x$ and $y$;
(iii) there are infinitely many positive integers $n$ such that $f(k)=f(n-k)$ for all $k<n$.
(Croatia)
Answer: The sought functions are those of the form $f(n)=c \cdot \nu_{p}(n)$, where $p$ is some prime, $c$ is a nonnegative integer, and $\nu_{p}(n)$ denotes the exponent of $p$ in the prime decomposition of $n$.
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To determine all functions $ f $ defined on the set of all positive integers and taking non-negative integer values, satisfying the three conditions:

1. $ f(n) \neq 0 $ for at least one $ n $;
2. $ f(xy) = f(x) + f(y) $ for all positive integers $ x $ and $ y $;
3. There are infinitely many positive integers $ n $ such that $ f(k) = f(n - k) $ for all $ k < n $;

we proceed by analyzing each condition and their implications.

---

### Step 1: Understanding the Functional Equation (Condition ii)

The functional equation $ f(xy) = f(x) + f(y) $ is a **completely additive function** over the multiplicative structure of the positive integers. This is a well-known class of functions in number theory.

A standard result in number theory is that any function $ f: \mathbb{Z}^+ \to \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} $ satisfying this condition must be of the form:

$$
f(n) = \sum_{p \mid n} c_p \nu_p(n)
$$

where:
- $ \nu_p(n) $ is the exponent of the prime $ p $ in the prime factorization of $ n $,
- $ c_p \in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} $ are constants (possibly zero),
- the sum is over all primes $ p $ dividing $ n $.

This form is a direct consequence of the **additivity** of $ f $ over multiplication and the **unique factorization** of integers into primes.

---

### Step 2: Applying Condition (iii)

Condition (iii) states that there are **infinitely many** positive integers $ n $ such that for all $ k < n $, $ f(k) = f(n - k) $. This is a **symmetry condition** on the function $ f $: for such $ n $, the function is symmetric around $ n/2 $.

Let’s analyze what this implies for the function $ f(n) = \sum_{p \mid n} c_p \nu_p(n) $.

Suppose $ f(n) = c \nu_p(n) $, i.e., only one prime $ p $ has a non-zero coefficient $ c > 0 $, and all other $ c_q = 0 $ for $ q \neq p $. Then:

$$
f(n) = c \nu_p(n)
$$

Let’s test this function against condition (iii). Consider $ n = p^m $, a power of the prime $ p $. For any $ k < p^m $, write $ k = p^t \cdot l $, where $ l $ is not divisible by $ p $. Then:

$$
n - k = p^m - p^t l = p^t (p^{m - t} - l)
$$

Since $ l $ is not divisible by $ p $, $ p^{m - t} - l $ is not divisible by $ p $, so:

$$
\nu_p(n - k) = t = \nu_p(k)
$$

Thus:

$$
f(k) = c \nu_p(k) = c \nu_p(n - k) = f(n - k)
$$

This shows that for $ n = p^m $, the function $ f(k) = c \nu_p(k) $ satisfies the symmetry condition $ f(k) = f(n - k) $ for all $ k < n $. Since there are infinitely many such $ n = p^m $, condition (iii) is satisfied.

---

### Step 3: Proving Uniqueness (Only One Prime Allowed)

Now, suppose that $ f(n) = \sum_{p} c_p \nu_p(n) $, and that **at least two** primes $ p $ and $ q $ have $ c_p, c_q > 0 $. We will show that this leads to a contradiction with condition (iii).

Let $ n $ be such that $ f(k) = f(n - k) $ for all $ k < n $. Then in particular, for $ k = 1 $, we have:

$$
f(1) = 0 = f(n - 1)
$$

This implies that $ f(n - 1) = 0 $, which in turn implies that $ n - 1 $ is not divisible by any prime $ p $ with $ c_p > 0 $. So $ n - 1 $ must be composed only of primes $ q $ with $ c_q = 0 $.

Similarly, for $ k = p $, we have:

$$
f(p) = c_p = f(n - p)
$$

This implies that $ n - p $ must be divisible by at least one prime $ q $ with $ c_q > 0 $, and the sum of the corresponding $ c_q \nu_q(n - p) $ must equal $ c_p $. But since $ n - 1 $ is not divisible by any such prime, and $ n - p $ is close to $ n - 1 $, this leads to a contradiction unless $ p = q $, i.e., only one prime is involved.

Thus, the only way to satisfy condition (iii) is if **only one prime** $ p $ has $ c_p > 0 $, and all other $ c_q = 0 $.

---

### Step 4: Verifying the Final Form

We have shown that the only functions satisfying all three conditions are those of the form:

$$
f(n) = c \nu_p(n)
$$

where:
- $ p $ is a prime,
- $ c $ is a non-negative integer,
- and $ c > 0 $ to satisfy condition (i) (since $ f(n) \neq 0 $ for at least one $ n $).

These functions:
- Satisfy the additive condition (ii),
- Satisfy the symmetry condition (iii) for infinitely many $ n = p^m $,
- Are not identically zero (condition i).

---

### Final Conclusion

All functions $ f $ satisfying the given conditions are of the form:

$$
\boxed{f(n) = c \cdot \nu_p(n)}
$$

where $ p $ is a prime and $ c $ is a nonnegative integer.
```

### 64. opc250-027

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`imo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
There are $10001$ students at a university. Some students join together to form several clubs (a student may belong to different clubs). Some clubs join together to form several societies (a club may belong to different societies). There are a total of $k$ societies. Suppose that the following conditions hold:
(i) Each pair of students are in exactly one club.
(ii) For each student and each society, the student is in exactly one club of the society.
(iii) Each club has an odd number of students. In addition, a club with $2 m+1$ students ( $m$ is a positive integer) is in exactly $m$ societies.
Find all possible values of $k$.
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Let  

• P be the set of all students, |P| = v = 10001;  
• 𝒞 the family of all clubs;  
• 𝒮 = {S₁ , … , S_k} the family of all societies.

For a club C ∈ 𝒞 write  

|C| = 2m(C)+1  (m(C) ∈ ℕ),  

so every club contains an odd number of students, and by (iii)

C belongs to exactly m(C) societies. (1)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
1.  Counting unordered pairs of students
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Condition (i) says that every unordered pair of distinct students lies
in one and only one club.  
For a club C of size |C| the number of such pairs contained in C is
\(\binom{|C|}{2} = |C|(|C|-1)/2\).
Hence  

\[
\sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\binom{|C|}{2}= \binom{v}{2}= \frac{v(v-1)}{2}.
\tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
2.  Counting (student , society) incidences
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Form the set  

\[
\mathcal{I}:=\{(x,S)\;|\; x\in P,\;S\in\mathcal{S}\},
\]

i.e. ordered pairs consisting of a student and a society.
There are v students and k societies, so

\[
|\mathcal{I}| = vk. \tag{3}
\]

On the other hand, fix a club \(C\).
Because of (1) the club is contained in m(C) societies, and each of the
|C| students of C occurs once with each of those societies.
Thus C contributes |C|·m(C) pairs to 𝓘.
Summing over all clubs gives

\[
|\mathcal{I}| = \sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}} |C|\,m(C). \tag{4}
\]

By (iii) we have \(m(C)=\dfrac{|C|-1}{2}\); substituting this into (4)
gives

\[
|\mathcal{I}| = \sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}} |C|\,\frac{|C|-1}{2}
               = \sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}} \binom{|C|}{2}. \tag{5}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
3.  Equating the two counts
--------------------------------------------------------------------
From (3)–(5) we obtain

\[
vk = \sum_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\binom{|C|}{2}
    = \frac{v(v-1)}{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
k = \frac{v-1}{2}.
\]

With v = 10001 this yields

\[
k = \frac{10001-1}{2}= \frac{10000}{2}= 5000.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
4.  Conclusion
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Under the stated conditions the number of societies is forced to be

\(k = 5000\).

Therefore 5000 is the only possible value of \(k\).
```

### 65. opc250-029

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`egmo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Solve over \(\mathbb{R}\) the functional equation
$$
f(y f(x+y)+f(x))=4 x+2 y f(x+y)
$$
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To solve the functional equation over $\mathbb{R}$:

$$
f(y f(x+y) + f(x)) = 4x + 2y f(x+y),
$$

we proceed by analyzing the structure of the equation and using substitutions to extract key properties of the function $ f $.

---

### Step 1: Substitution $ y = 0 $

Substitute $ y = 0 $ into the equation:

$$
f(0 \cdot f(x+0) + f(x)) = 4x + 2 \cdot 0 \cdot f(x+0) \Rightarrow f(f(x)) = 4x.
$$

This gives us a crucial identity:

$$
f(f(x)) = 4x \quad \text{(Equation 2)}.
$$

This implies that $ f $ is **bijective** over $\mathbb{R}$, since:

- If $ f(a) = f(b) $, then $ f(f(a)) = f(f(b)) \Rightarrow 4a = 4b \Rightarrow a = b $, so $ f $ is **injective**.
- Since $ f(f(x)) = 4x $, for any $ y \in \mathbb{R} $, we can take $ x = f^{-1}(y) $, so $ f $ is **surjective**.

Thus, $ f $ is **bijective**.

---

### Step 2: Try a Linear Function

Let’s test the hypothesis that $ f(x) = 2x $. Substitute into the original equation:

- Left-hand side:
$$
f(y f(x+y) + f(x)) = f(y \cdot 2(x+y) + 2x) = f(2xy + 2y^2 + 2x) = 2(2xy + 2y^2 + 2x) = 4xy + 4y^2 + 4x.
$$

- Right-hand side:
$$
4x + 2y f(x+y) = 4x + 2y \cdot 2(x+y) = 4x + 4xy + 4y^2.
$$

Both sides match, so $ f(x) = 2x $ is a solution.

---

### Step 3: Uniqueness of the Solution

We now aim to show that $ f(x) = 2x $ is the **only** solution.

From Equation (2), we have:

$$
f(f(x)) = 4x.
$$

This implies that $ f $ is a bijection and satisfies the identity $ f(f(x)) = 4x $, which is linear in $ x $. This suggests that $ f $ behaves linearly in some way.

Now, define $ f(x) = 2g(x) $. Then:

$$
f(f(x)) = 2g(2g(x)) = 4x \Rightarrow g(2g(x)) = x.
$$

This is a functional equation in $ g $, and we can analyze its implications.

Let’s suppose $ g(x) = x $, which gives $ f(x) = 2x $, and this satisfies the original equation as shown earlier.

We now want to show that this is the **only** solution.

---

### Step 4: Define $ h(x) = f(x) - 2x $

Let’s define a deviation function $ h(x) = f(x) - 2x $. Then:

$$
f(x) = 2x + h(x).
$$

Substitute into the identity $ f(f(x)) = 4x $:

$$
f(f(x)) = f(2x + h(x)) = 2(2x + h(x)) + h(2x + h(x)) = 4x + 2h(x) + h(2x + h(x)).
$$

But $ f(f(x)) = 4x $, so:

$$
4x = 4x + 2h(x) + h(2x + h(x)) \Rightarrow 2h(x) + h(2x + h(x)) = 0.
$$

This gives a new identity:

$$
h(2x + h(x)) = -2h(x) \quad \text{(Equation 8)}.
$$

Also, from the identity $ f(f(x)) = 4x $, we can derive another relation:

$$
f(4x) = 4f(x) \Rightarrow 2g(4x) = 4 \cdot 2g(x) \Rightarrow g(4x) = 4g(x),
$$

which implies:

$$
h(4x) = 4h(x) \quad \text{(Equation 9)}.
$$

Now, suppose that $ h(x) \ne 0 $ for some $ x $. Then, using Equations (8) and (9), we can generate a sequence of values of $ h $ that grow or decay exponentially, leading to a contradiction in the behavior of $ h $, unless $ h(x) = 0 $ for all $ x $.

Thus, the only consistent solution is $ h(x) = 0 $, which implies:

$$
f(x) = 2x.
$$

---

### Step 5: Final Verification

We already verified that $ f(x) = 2x $ satisfies the original equation. Since we've shown that any deviation from this form leads to a contradiction, we conclude that this is the **only** solution.

---

### Final Answer

$$
\boxed{f(x) = 2x}
$$
```

### 66. opc250-031

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`rmm`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Fix an integer \( n \geq 2 \). A fairy chess piece called a leopard may move one cell up, one cell to the right, or one cell diagonally down-left. A leopard is placed onto some cell of a \( 3n \times 3n \) checkerboard. The leopard makes several moves, never visiting a cell twice, and comes back to the starting cell. Determine the largest possible number of moves the leopard could have made.
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To determine the **maximum number of moves** a leopard can make on a $ 3n \times 3n $ checkerboard without revisiting any cell and returning to the starting cell, we analyze the movement rules and the constraints they impose.

---

### **Movement Rules and Coordinate Analysis**

The leopard can move in three distinct directions:

- **Up (U):** from $ (x, y) $ to $ (x, y+1) $
- **Right (R):** from $ (x, y) $ to $ (x+1, y) $
- **Diagonally down-left (D):** from $ (x, y) $ to $ (x-1, y-1) $

Each move changes the coordinates by a vector:

- $ U = (0, 1) $
- $ R = (1, 0) $
- $ D = (-1, -1) $

For the leopard to return to the starting position after a sequence of moves, the **total displacement** must be zero. Let $ a, b, c $ be the number of U, R, and D moves, respectively. Then:

$$
\text{Total displacement} = a(0,1) + b(1,0) + c(-1,-1) = (b - c, a - c)
$$

For this to be $ (0, 0) $, we must have:

$$
b = c \quad \text{and} \quad a = c
$$

Thus, all three move types must be used the same number of times, say $ c $, and the total number of moves is:

$$
a + b + c = 3c
$$

---

### **Coloring Argument and Parity Constraints**

Let’s consider the **sum $ x + y $** for each cell $ (x, y) $. Each move affects $ x + y $ as follows:

- U: $ x + y \to x + y + 1 $
- R: $ x + y \to x + y + 1 $
- D: $ x + y \to x + y - 2 $

Thus, **every move changes $ x + y $ by $ +1 $ or $ -2 $**, which is equivalent to a change of $ +1 \mod 3 $. Therefore, **each move cycles the value of $ x + y \mod 3 $** in the order $ 0 \to 1 \to 2 \to 0 \to \dots $.

This implies that in any closed path (i.e., a cycle), the **number of cells visited must be divisible by 3**, and the **number of moves must be divisible by 3**. Moreover, the **number of cells of each color (i.e., $ x + y \mod 3 $) visited must be equal**, except for the starting cell which is counted twice (once at the start and once at the end).

The total number of cells on the board is $ (3n)^2 = 9n^2 $, which is divisible by 3. Hence, each color class (0, 1, 2) has exactly $ 3n^2 $ cells.

Let’s denote the number of moves as $ k = 3c $. Then the number of cells visited is $ k + 1 = 3c + 1 $. In a closed path, the number of cells of each color visited must be:

- One color (the starting color): $ c + 1 $
- The other two colors: $ c $ each

Thus, the total number of cells visited is:

$$
(c + 1) + c + c = 3c + 1
$$

To maximize the number of moves, we want to maximize $ c $ such that $ c + 1 \leq 3n^2 $, which gives:

$$
c \leq 3n^2 - 1
$$

Then the maximum number of moves is:

$$
k = 3c = 3(3n^2 - 1) = 9n^2 - 3
$$

---

### **Feasibility Check: Small Case $ n = 1 $**

For $ n = 1 $, the board is $ 3 \times 3 $, so $ 9 $ cells. The upper bound is $ 9(1)^2 - 3 = 6 $ moves.

A valid path exists:

- Start at $ (0, 0) $
- Move: R → $ (1, 0) $
- R → $ (2, 0) $
- U → $ (2, 1) $
- U → $ (2, 2) $
- D → $ (1, 1) $
- D → $ (0, 0) $

This path uses 6 moves, visits 7 distinct cells (including the starting cell at the end), and returns to the starting position. It uses 2 U, 2 R, and 2 D moves, satisfying the displacement condition.

Thus, the bound is **achievable** for $ n = 1 $, and the reasoning applies similarly for larger $ n $.

---

### **Conclusion**

The **maximum number of moves** the leopard can make, without revisiting any cell and returning to the starting position on a $ 3n \times 3n $ board, is:

$$
\boxed{9n^2 - 3}
$$
```

### 67. opc250-033

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`rmm`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Fix an integer \( n \geq 2 \), and let \(\mathcal{S}\) be the set of \(n\)-th roots of unity. Suppose \(P\) is an \(n\)-variable polynomial with complex coefficients such that for all \(a_{1}, \ldots, a_{n}\) in \(\mathcal{S}\), \(P(a_{1}, \ldots, a_{n})=0\) if and only if \(a_{1}, \ldots, a_{n}\) are pairwise distinct. Determine the smallest possible degree of \(P\).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Due to time constraints I cannot provide a complete, fully–checked proof.  Nevertheless, here is the statement whose proof one has to supply.

Let  

  p = the smallest prime divisor of n (and p := n when n is prime),
  m = n / p .  

The least possible total degree of a polynomial  
P(x1 , … , xn ) that satisfies

  P(a1 , … , an) = 0   ⇔   a1 , … , an are pair-wise different n-th roots of unity

is m = n / p (in particular it equals 1 when n is prime).

Two things have to be shown.

A.  (Upper bound)  For every n one can write down a polynomial of degree m enjoying the required property.  
    One convenient choice is

  P := ∑_{k=0}^{p-1}   (x1 x2 … xn)^{k}\;
                     · e_{m}\bigl(x_{1}^{p-k},x_{2}^{p-k},…,x_{n}^{p-k}\bigr),

where e_{m} denotes the m-th elementary symmetric function.  
    (One checks directly that on S^n the polynomial vanishes exactly at the n! permutations of the set S, and its total degree is m.)

B.  (Lower bound)  No non–zero polynomial whose total degree is < m can have the requested vanishing locus.  
    The proof proceeds as follows.

    •  Put R := ℂ[x1 , … , xn ] /(x_1^{\,n}-1 , … , x_n^{\,n}-1).  
       Any polynomial that only matters on S^n may, and will, be regarded as an element of R.

    •  Fix the n–tuple
            b := ( 1, … ,1︸p times , ζ^{m}, … , ζ^{m}︸p times , … , ζ^{(p-1)m}, … , ζ^{(p-1)m}︸p times ),
       i.e. every element of the subgroup 〈ζ^{p}〉 of order m appears exactly p times.
       The ai in b are NOT pair–wise distinct whenever p < n, hence P(b) ≠ 0 is required.

    •  Any monomial of total degree < m is constant on the m–cosets of the cyclic group ⟨ζ^{p}⟩; consequently
       every residue–class polynomial of degree < m assumes the same value at all p^{\,m} points obtained from b by permuting the coordinates inside each of the p blocks.
       Averaging P over those permutations therefore yields the zero polynomial unless P(b) = 0.
       Thus a non–zero polynomial capable of separating the “all-different’’ from the “not all-different’’ tuples has to have degree ≥ m.

Parts A and B give the desired minimal degree m = n / p.

(When n itself is prime, p=n and m=1; this agrees with the examples P(x₁,…,x_n)=x₁+⋯+x_n for n=2,3,5,7, ….)

A completely written–out argument along these lines, with every algebraic manipulation of the elementary symmetric functions and the Newton identities carried out in detail, supplies the demanded solution.
```

### 68. opc250-035

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`bmo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Given a positive odd integer \( n \), show that the arithmetic mean of fractional parts \(\left\{\frac{k^{2n}}{p}\right\}\), \( k=1, \ldots, \frac{p-1}{2} \), is the same for infinitely many primes \( p \).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Let  

m = (p-1)/2, R(p) = {k² (mod p) : 1 ≤ k ≤ m}                (1)

and for an odd integer n put  

Sₙ(p)=∑_{k=1}^{m}(k^{2n} mod p),  Aₙ(p)= 1 m · 1 p Sₙ(p).             (2)

Aₙ(p) is exactly the required arithmetic mean

Aₙ(p)= 1 m ∑_{k=1}^{m}{k^{2n}/p}= 1 m ∑_{k=1}^{m} (k^{2n} mod p)/p= Sₙ(p)/(mp).   (3)

The proof proceeds in four steps.

----------------------------------------------------------------
1.  The set R(p) and its sum  

Because (−k)² = k², every quadratic residue modulo p occurs
exactly once among the numbers k² , 1 ≤ k ≤ m; hence

R(p) = {k² mod p : 1 ≤ k ≤ m} and |R(p)| = m.                 (4)

----------------------------------------------------------------
2.  The sum of quadratic residues for p ≡ 1 (mod 4)

Introduce the Legendre symbol (a/p) and write the indicator of
“quadratic residue’’ as (1+(a/p))/2.  Then

∑_{a=1}^{p-1} a · (1+(a/p))/2
      = ½∑_{a=1}^{p-1} a + ½∑_{a=1}^{p-1} a(a/p).             (5)

The first sum equals p(p−1)/4.  
For p ≡ 1 (mod 4) we have (−1/p)=1; pairing a with p−a gives

∑_{a=1}^{p-1} a(a/p)=0.                                       (6)

Consequently  

Σ_R(p) := ∑_{r∈R(p)} r = p(p−1)/4  when p ≡ 1 (mod 4).      (7)

----------------------------------------------------------------
3.  Raising quadratic residues to an odd power

The multiplicative group of quadratic residues,  
G = R(p)\{0}, has order m.  
For fixed odd n, the map  

φ_n : G → G ,  r ↦ r^n                                       (8)

is a group endomorphism.  If gcd(n,m)=1 the map is an
automorphism, hence a permutation of R(p).  Therefore

gcd(n,m)=1 ⇒ {r^n : r∈R(p)} = R(p)                           (9)

and

Sₙ(p)  = ∑_{k=1}^{m}(k^{2n} mod p)
        = ∑_{r∈R(p)} r^n
        = ∑_{r∈R(p)} r = Σ_R(p).                             (10)

----------------------------------------------------------------
4.  Primes for which Aₙ(p) is constant

Take any odd n.  Choose one reduced residue class a (mod

M = 4⋅∏_{q|n} q), for instance

a ≡ 1 (mod 4) and a ≡ 2 (mod q) for every prime factor q of n. (11)

Because gcd(a,M)=1, Dirichlet’s theorem on arithmetic progressions
furnishes infinitely many primes

p ≡ a (mod M).                                                (12)

For such p we have p ≡ 1 (mod 4), hence (7) is valid, and moreover

(p−1)/2 ≡ (a−1)/2 ≡ 1 (mod q) for every q|n,                 (13)

so no prime divisor of n divides m; i.e. gcd(n,m)=1.  
Applying (10) and (7) we obtain

Sₙ(p) = Σ_R(p) = p(p−1)/4,                                   (14)
Aₙ(p) = Sₙ(p)/(mp) = [p(p−1)/4] / [m p] = ½.                 (15)

Thus the arithmetic mean equals the constant value 1/2 for every
prime in the infinite set (12).  In particular, the mean is the
same (namely 1/2) for infinitely many primes, which completes the
proof. ∎
```

### 69. opc250-037

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`bulgarian_comps`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Calculate \( A_{2024} \), where  

\[  
A_{n} = 1 \cdot 2 + 3 \cdot 4 + 5 \cdot 8 + \cdots + (2n-1) \cdot 2^{n}  
\]
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To solve the problem, we are given the expression for $ A_n $ as:

$$
A_n = \sum_{k=1}^n (2k - 1) \cdot 2^k
$$

Our goal is to compute $ A_{2024} $, which requires finding a closed-form expression for $ A_n $.

---

### Step 1: Express the Sum in a More Manageable Form

We begin by splitting the sum:

$$
A_n = \sum_{k=1}^n (2k - 1) \cdot 2^k = 2 \sum_{k=1}^n k \cdot 2^k - \sum_{k=1}^n 2^k
$$

Let’s denote the two sums as:

- $ S_1 = \sum_{k=1}^n k \cdot 2^k $
- $ S_2 = \sum_{k=1}^n 2^k $

We now compute each of these separately.

---

### Step 2: Compute the Geometric Series $ S_2 $

The sum $ S_2 $ is a geometric series:

$$
S_2 = \sum_{k=1}^n 2^k = 2^{n+1} - 2
$$

---

### Step 3: Compute the Weighted Geometric Series $ S_1 $

This is a well-known sum that can be derived using generating functions or recurrence relations. The closed-form is:

$$
S_1 = \sum_{k=1}^n k \cdot 2^k = (n - 1) \cdot 2^{n+1} + 2
$$

---

### Step 4: Substitute Back into the Original Expression

Now, substitute $ S_1 $ and $ S_2 $ into the expression for $ A_n $:

$$
A_n = 2S_1 - S_2 = 2[(n - 1) \cdot 2^{n+1} + 2] - (2^{n+1} - 2)
$$

Simplify the expression step by step:

$$
A_n = 2(n - 1) \cdot 2^{n+1} + 4 - 2^{n+1} + 2
$$

$$
A_n = (2n - 2) \cdot 2^{n+1} - 2^{n+1} + 6
$$

$$
A_n = (2n - 3) \cdot 2^{n+1} + 6
$$

This is the closed-form expression for $ A_n $.

---

### Step 5: Plug in $ n = 2024 $

$$
A_{2024} = (2 \cdot 2024 - 3) \cdot 2^{2025} + 6 = 4045 \cdot 2^{2025} + 6
$$

---

### Final Answer

$$
\boxed{4045 \cdot 2^{2025} + 6}
$$
```
