### 128. opc250-155

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`bmo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Prove that there is no function from positive real numbers to itself, \( f:(0,+\infty) \rightarrow(0,+\infty) \) such that:

\[
f(f(x)+y)=f(x)+3x+yf(y) \quad \text{for every} \quad x, y \in(0,+\infty)
\]
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To prove that **no function** $ f: (0, +\infty) \to (0, +\infty) $ satisfies the equation

$$
f(f(x) + y) = f(x) + 3x + y f(y)
$$

for all $ x, y \in (0, +\infty) $, we proceed by analyzing the properties of the function $ f $ and deriving a contradiction.

---

### Step 1: Prove that $ f $ is injective

Assume $ f(a) = f(b) $. Then, for any $ y > 0 $, we have:

$$
f(f(a) + y) = f(f(b) + y)
$$

Using the functional equation on both sides:

$$
f(f(a) + y) = f(a) + 3a + y f(y)
$$
$$
f(f(b) + y) = f(b) + 3b + y f(y)
$$

Since $ f(a) = f(b) $, the left-hand sides are equal, and so are the right-hand sides. Thus:

$$
f(a) + 3a + y f(y) = f(b) + 3b + y f(y)
$$

Subtracting $ f(a) = f(b) $ and $ y f(y) $ from both sides, we get:

$$
3a = 3b \Rightarrow a = b
$$

Therefore, $ f $ is **injective**.

---

### Step 2: Analyze the functional equation and derive a contradiction

Let us denote $ z = f(x) $, so the equation becomes:

$$
f(z + y) = z + 3x + y f(y)
$$

This suggests that the value of $ f(z + y) $ is determined by $ z $, $ x $, and $ y $. But since $ z = f(x) $, and $ f $ is injective, we can express $ x $ in terms of $ z $ (i.e., $ x = f^{-1}(z) $), assuming $ f $ is surjective. However, we do **not** need surjectivity to proceed.

Now, consider the difference:

$$
f(z + y) - f(z + w) = (z + 3x + y f(y)) - (z + 3x + w f(w)) = y f(y) - w f(w)
$$

So, for any $ z > 0 $, the difference $ f(z + y) - f(z + w) $ depends only on $ y $ and $ w $, **not on $ z $**. This is a strong condition.

Let us fix $ w = 1 $, and define:

$$
f(z + y) - f(z + 1) = y f(y) - f(1)
$$

This equation must hold for all $ z > 0 $, and for all $ y > 0 $. Therefore, the **difference $ f(z + y) - f(z + 1) $ is constant with respect to $ z $**.

This implies that the function $ f $ has the property that the difference $ f(z + h) - f(z) $ is **independent of $ z $** for any fixed $ h $. This is a well-known condition in functional equations: if a function $ f $ satisfies

$$
f(z + h) - f(z) = k(h)
$$

for all $ z > 0 $ and some function $ k $, then $ k $ is additive over $ h $, and $ f $ must be **linear** (i.e., $ f(z) = m z + c $) under mild regularity conditions (e.g., continuity or monotonicity).

Indeed, we can define $ k(h) = f(z + h) - f(z) $, and then for any $ h_1, h_2 > 0 $:

$$
k(h_1 + h_2) = f(z + h_1 + h_2) - f(z) = [f(z + h_1 + h_2) - f(z + h_1)] + [f(z + h_1) - f(z)] = k(h_2) + k(h_1)
$$

So $ k $ is additive, and since $ f $ maps $ (0, \infty) \to (0, \infty) $, $ k(h) > 0 $ for all $ h > 0 $. The only additive function on $ (0, \infty) $ that is positive and bounded below on any interval is a linear function $ k(h) = m h $, with $ m > 0 $.

Therefore, $ f(z) = m z + c $ for some constants $ m > 0 $, $ c \in \mathbb{R} $.

---

### Step 3: Show that no linear function satisfies the original equation

Assume $ f(x) = m x + c $, with $ m > 0 $, $ c \geq 0 $ (to ensure $ f(x) > 0 $ for all $ x > 0 $).

Substitute into the original equation:

**Left-hand side:**

$$
f(f(x) + y) = f(m x + c + y) = m(m x + c + y) + c = m^2 x + m c + m y + c
$$

**Right-hand side:**

$$
f(x) + 3x + y f(y) = (m x + c) + 3x + y(m y + c) = (m + 3) x + c + m y^2 + c y
$$

Equating both sides:

$$
m^2 x + m y + (m c + c) = (m + 3) x + m y^2 + c y + c
$$

Simplify:

$$
(m^2 - m - 3) x + (m - c) y - m y^2 + (m c) = 0
$$

This equation must hold for all $ x, y > 0 $. Therefore, the coefficients of $ x $, $ y $, and $ y^2 $ must all be zero:

- $ m^2 - m - 3 = 0 $
- $ m - c = 0 $
- $ -m = 0 $

From the last equation, $ m = 0 $, which contradicts $ m > 0 $. Therefore, **no such linear function** can satisfy the original equation.

---

### Step 4: Conclude the contradiction

We have shown that:

- If such a function $ f $ exists, it must be injective.
- The functional equation implies that $ f $ must be linear.
- But no linear function satisfies the original equation.

Therefore, **no such function** $ f: (0, +\infty) \to (0, +\infty) $ exists that satisfies the given functional equation.

---

### Final Answer

$$
\boxed{\text{No such function exists.}}
$$
```

### 129. opc250-157

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`egmo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
The number \(2021\) is fantabulous. For any positive integer \(m\), if any element of the set \(\{m, 2m+1, 3m\}\) is fantabulous, then all the elements are fantabulous. Does it follow that the number \(2021^{2021}\) is fantabulous?
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To determine whether the number $ 2021^{2021} $ is **fantabulous**, we must understand the implications of the given rule:

> For any positive integer $ m $, if **any** element of the set $ \{m, 2m+1, 3m\} $ is fantabulous, then **all** elements of that set are fantabulous.

This rule defines a **closure property** for the set of fantabulous numbers. Starting from the known fact that $ 2021 $ is fantabulous, we can generate other fantabulous numbers by applying the rule in both directions:

- **Forward direction**: If $ m $ is fantabulous, then $ 2m+1 $ and $ 3m $ are also fantabulous.
- **Backward direction**: If $ 2m+1 $ or $ 3m $ is fantabulous, then $ m $ must be fantabulous.

---

### Step 1: Understanding the Structure of Fantabulous Numbers

Let’s denote the set of all fantabulous numbers as $ F $. The rule implies that $ F $ is the **smallest** set containing $ 2021 $ and closed under the following operations:

- If $ m \in F $, then $ 2m+1 \in F $ and $ 3m \in F $.
- If $ 2m+1 \in F $, then $ m \in F $.
- If $ 3m \in F $, then $ m \in F $.

This means that $ F $ is the **closure** of $ \{2021\} $ under the operations $ m \mapsto 2m+1 $, $ m \mapsto 3m $, and their inverses (when defined).

---

### Step 2: Attempting to Reach $ 2021^{2021} $

We are asked whether $ 2021^{2021} $ is in $ F $, i.e., whether it can be generated from $ 2021 $ using the allowed operations.

Let’s denote $ N = 2021^{2021} $. Since $ N $ is **odd**, we can attempt to reduce it using the inverse of the $ 2m+1 $ operation:

$$
N_1 = \frac{N - 1}{2}
$$

This is an integer because $ N $ is odd. However, $ N_1 $ is **even**, and since $ N $ is not divisible by 3 (as $ 2021 \equiv 2 \mod 3 $), $ N_1 $ is also not divisible by 3.

This means that $ N_1 $ cannot be reduced further using the inverse of the $ 3m $ operation. The only way $ N_1 $ could be in $ F $ is if it is the **base** of a set $ \{N_1, 2N_1 + 1, 3N_1\} $, and at least one of the other two elements is in $ F $.

But:

- $ 2N_1 + 1 = N $, which is what we are trying to prove.
- $ 3N_1 = \frac{3(N - 1)}{2} $, which is not obviously in $ F $.

So we are stuck in a **circular dependency**: to prove $ N \in F $, we need $ N_1 \in F $, which in turn requires $ 3N_1 \in F $, and so on.

---

### Step 3: Exploring the Structure of $ F $

Let’s consider the **structure** of $ F $:

- Starting from $ 2021 $, we can generate numbers like $ 2 \cdot 2021 + 1 = 4043 $, $ 3 \cdot 2021 = 6063 $, and so on.
- From $ 4043 $, we can generate $ 2 \cdot 4043 + 1 = 8087 $, $ 3 \cdot 4043 = 12129 $, etc.
- From $ 6063 $, we can generate $ 2 \cdot 6063 + 1 = 12127 $, $ 3 \cdot 6063 = 18189 $, etc.

We can also go **downward** from $ 2021 $, since $ 2021 = 2 \cdot 1010 + 1 $, so $ 1010 $ must be in $ F $.

From $ 1010 $, we can generate $ 2 \cdot 1010 + 1 = 2021 $, $ 3 \cdot 1010 = 3030 $, and so on.

So $ F $ includes:

- $ 2021, 1010, 3030, 4043, 6063, 8087, 12127, 12129, 18189, \dots $

But **none of these numbers** are equal to $ 2021^{2021} $, and none of the operations allow us to reach such a large power of 2021.

---

### Step 4: Inductive Attempt

Suppose we try to prove by induction that $ 2021^k \in F $ for all $ k \geq 1 $.

- **Base case**: $ 2021^1 = 2021 \in F $, which is given.
- **Inductive step**: Assume $ 2021^k \in F $. Then $ 2 \cdot 2021^k + 1 $ and $ 3 \cdot 2021^k $ are in $ F $, but neither is equal to $ 2021^{k+1} $.

So the inductive step **fails**, because $ 2021^{k+1} $ is not directly generated by the allowed operations from $ 2021^k $.

---

### Step 5: Conclusion

We have shown that:

- $ 2021^{2021} $ is **odd**, so it can be reduced to $ N_1 = \frac{2021^{2021} - 1}{2} $, which is **even** and **not divisible by 3**.
- $ N_1 $ cannot be reduced further using the inverse of the $ 3m $ operation.
- $ N_1 $ is not obviously in $ F $, and we cannot find a path from $ N_1 $ to any known fantabulous number.
- Inductive attempts to build up to $ 2021^{2021} $ from $ 2021 $ fail.

Therefore, **we cannot conclude** that $ 2021^{2021} $ is in $ F $, and thus, it does **not** follow that $ 2021^{2021} $ is fantabulous.

---

### Final Answer

$$
\boxed{No}
$$
```

### 130. opc250-159

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`bmo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \( n \geq 3 \) be an integer and let

\[
M=\left\{\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}+\cdots+a_{k}}{k}: 1 \leq k \leq n \text{ and } 1 \leq a_{1}<\cdots<a_{k} \leq n\right\}
\]

be the set of the arithmetic means of the elements of all non-empty subsets of \(\{1,2, \ldots, n\}\). Find \(\min \{|a-b|: a, b \in M \text{ with } a \neq b\}\).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Answer. 
 For every integer \(n\ge 3\)

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle 
\min _{\;a\ne b\in M}|a-b|=\frac1{(n-1)(\,n-2\,)} } .
\]

We give a complete proof in two parts:  

(1) an explicit pair of subsets whose means differ by the claimed value, and  

(2) a proof that no two different means can be closer than that.

Throughout we write  

\[
T=n(n+1)/2=\sum_{i=1}^{n}i ,
\qquad 
\{1,2,\dots ,n\}=:[n].
\]


--------------------------------------------------------------------
1.  An explicit gap equal to \(\dfrac1{(n-1)(n-2)}\)
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Step 1.1 Choose a subset of size \(n-1\).  
Put 
\[
x:=\bigl(T\bmod (n-1)\bigr)+1 ,
\qquad 
B:=[n]\setminus\{x\}\quad(|B|=n-1).
\]
Because \(1\le T\bmod (n-1)\le n-2\), the number \(x\) satisfies \(1\le x\le n\).

The sum of the elements of \(B\) is 
\[
S_B=T-x\equiv -1\pmod{\,n-1}.
\tag{1}
\]

Step 1.2 Produce an integer which will be the sum of an \((n-2)\)-subset.  
Because \(n-2\equiv-1\pmod{n-1}\), from (1)

\[
(n-2)S_B-1\equiv(-1)(-1)-1\equiv0\pmod{n-1},
\]

hence  

\[
s:=\frac{(n-2)S_B-1}{n-1}\in\mathbb Z .
\tag{2}
\]

Step 1.3 Show that \(s\) can be realised as the sum of \(n-2\) distinct numbers of \([n]\).  
For \(k\;(1\le k\le n)\) put  

\[
\underline S_k=\frac{k(k+1)}2 ,\qquad 
\overline S_k=\frac{k(2n-k+1)}2 ,
\]

the minimal and maximal possible sums of \(k\)-element subsets of \([n]\).
A classical “shifting–greedy’’ argument (see Lemma A below) shows that every integer between
\(\underline S_k\) and \(\overline S_k\) is attained by some \(k\)-subset.

For \(k=n-2\) we have  

\[
\underline S_{n-2}=\frac{(n-2)(n-1)}2,\qquad 
\overline S_{n-2}=T-3.
\]

Because \(x\ge1\), from (2)

\[
s=\frac{(n-2)(T-x)-1}{n-1}\le\frac{(n-2)(T-1)-1}{n-1}
            =T-3=\overline S_{n-2}.
\]

Moreover \(x\le n\) gives  

\[
s\ge\frac{(n-2)(T-n)-1}{n-1}
      =\frac{(n-2)(n-1)}2=\underline S_{n-2}.
\]

Hence \(s\) indeed lies between the extreme sums, so by Lemma A there exists an \((n-2)\)-subset \(A\subset[n]\) with \(\sum_{a\in A}a=s\).

Step 1.4 The required difference.  
With \(|A|=n-2,\ |B|=n-1\) and (2),

\[
(n-1)\sum_{a\in A}a-(n-2)\sum_{b\in B}b
      =(n-1)s-(n-2)S_B=1 .
\]

Therefore  

\[
\Bigl|\;\frac{\sum_{a\in A}a}{n-2}-\frac{\sum_{b\in B}b}{n-1}\Bigr|
      =\frac1{(n-1)(n-2)} .
\tag{3}
\]

So the minimal distance is at most the claimed value.



--------------------------------------------------------------------
2.  No two different means can be closer
--------------------------------------------------------------------

Take any two different means coming from subsets \(P,Q\subset[n]\) of sizes  

\[
|P|=k,\quad |Q|=t,\qquad 1\le k<t\le n.
\]

Write their sums \(\Sigma_P,\Sigma_Q\).  Their difference is  

\[
\Bigl|\frac{\Sigma_P}{k}-\frac{\Sigma_Q}{t}\Bigr|
   =\frac{|t\Sigma_P-k\Sigma_Q|}{kt}
   =:\frac d{kt},\qquad d\in\mathbb N.
\tag{4}
\]

Step 2.1 If \(d\ge2\).  
Because \(k\le n-1,\ t\le n\),

\[
\frac d{kt}\ge\frac2{n(n-1)}\ge
   \frac1{(n-1)(n-2)}\quad(n\ge4),
\]

and for \(n=3\) we have \(2/ (3\cdot2)=1/3>1/2\).  
Hence a value coming from \(d\ge2\) cannot beat (3).

Step 2.2 Hence we only have to treat the case \(d=1\).  

(i)  Then \(\gcd(k,t)=1\) (since the numerator in (4) is divisible by \(\gcd(k,t)\)).  

(ii)  If \(t=n\) (i.e. one of the subsets is the whole set) then  
\[
t\Sigma_P-k\Sigma_Q
      =n\Sigma_P-kT
\]
is divisible by \(n\;(\ge3)\), contradicting \(d=1\).  Thus \(t\le n-1\).

(iii)  Because \(k<t\le n-1\) and \(k,t\) are coprime, the largest possible product is  
\(k=t-1=n-2\).  Consequently  
\[
kt\le(n-2)(n-1).
\]

Step 2.3 With \(d=1\) and the preceding inequality, (4) gives  

\[
\Bigl|\frac{\Sigma_P}{k}-\frac{\Sigma_Q}{t}\Bigr|
        =\frac1{kt}\ge\frac1{(n-1)(n-2)}.
\]

Combining this with Step 2.1, every two different means are at least
\(1/((n-1)(n-2))\) apart.  Because (3) shows that this distance is in fact attained, it is the minimum.



--------------------------------------------------------------------
Lemma A  (Completeness of the range of \(k\)-subset sums)
--------------------------------------------------------------------
For fixed \(n\) and \(k\;(1\le k\le n)\) every integer between  

\[
\underline S_k=\frac{k(k+1)}2
\quad\text{and}\quad
\overline S_k=\frac{k(2n-k+1)}2
\]

occurs as the sum of a \(k\)-element subset of \([n]\).

Proof.  
Write a desired sum as \(\underline S_k+\delta\ (0\le\delta\le k(n-k))\).
Distribute \(\delta\) among the \(k\) numbers \(1,2,\dots ,k\) by choosing
non-negative integers  

\[
0\le d_1\le d_2\le\cdots\le d_k\le n-k ,
\qquad d_1+\cdots+d_k=\delta .
\]

(This is always possible by a simple greedy procedure.)  
The \(k\)-tuple \((1+d_1,2+d_2,\dots ,k+d_k)\) consists of distinct integers
between \(1\) and \(n\); its sum is the required value. ∎



--------------------------------------------------------------------
3.  Small cases
--------------------------------------------------------------------
For completeness we record the first two cases:

• \(n=3\):  the means are  
\(1,\,\tfrac32,\,2,\,\tfrac52,\,3\);  the minimal gap is \(1/2\), which equals \(1/((3-1)(3-2))\).

• \(n=4\):  the pair  
\(\{2,3\}\) (mean \(5/2\)) and \(\{1,2,4\}\) (mean \(7/3\)) realises the gap \(1/6=1/((4-1)(4-2))\).

Thus the formula proved above is valid for every \(n\ge3\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Conclusion
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Both the construction in Part 1 and the bound in Part 2 give

\[
\min_{a\ne b\in M}|a-b|=\frac1{(n-1)(n-2)},
\]

establishing the required result. ∎
```

### 131. opc250-161

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`egmo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \( n \) be a positive integer.

(a) Prove that there exists a set \( S \) of \( 6n \) positive integers such that the least common multiple of any two is at most \( 32n^2 \).

(b) Show that every set \( T \) of \( 6n \) positive integers contains two elements with least common multiple exceeding \( 9n^2 \).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：### **Problem (a): Constructing a Set with Bounded LCMs**

We are to prove that there exists a set $ S $ of $ 6n $ positive integers such that the least common multiple (LCM) of any two elements in $ S $ is at most $ 32n^2 $.

---

#### **Construction of the Set $ S $**

Let us define the set $ S $ as follows:

$$
S = \{ k \mid n+1 \leq k \leq 4n \} \cup \{ 2k \mid n+1 \leq k \leq 4n \}
$$

This set contains:

- $ 3n $ elements from the range $ n+1 $ to $ 4n $,
- $ 3n $ elements from the range $ 2(n+1) $ to $ 8n $ (i.e., $ 2k $ for $ k \in [n+1, 4n] $).

Thus, $ |S| = 6n $, as required.

---

#### **Bounding the LCM of Any Two Elements in $ S $**

We analyze the LCM of any two elements in $ S $, considering all possible combinations:

1. **Case 1: Both elements are in $ \{k \mid n+1 \leq k \leq 4n\} $:**

   Let $ a, b \in [n+1, 4n] $. Then:
   $$
   \text{LCM}(a, b) = \frac{ab}{\gcd(a, b)} \leq ab \leq (4n)^2 = 16n^2
   $$

2. **Case 2: One element is in $ \{k\} $, the other in $ \{2k\} $:**

   Let $ a = k $, $ b = 2m $, where $ k, m \in [n+1, 4n] $. Then:
   $$
   \text{LCM}(k, 2m) = \frac{k \cdot 2m}{\gcd(k, 2m)}
   $$
   - If $ k $ is even, then $ \gcd(k, 2m) \geq 2 $, so:
     $$
     \text{LCM}(k, 2m) \leq \frac{k \cdot 2m}{2} = km \leq (4n)^2 = 16n^2
     $$
   - If $ k $ is odd, then $ \gcd(k, 2m) = \gcd(k, m) $, so:
     $$
     \text{LCM}(k, 2m) = \frac{2km}{\gcd(k, m)} \leq 2km \leq 2 \cdot (4n)^2 = 32n^2
     $$

3. **Case 3: Both elements are in $ \{2k\} $:**

   Let $ a = 2k $, $ b = 2m $, where $ k, m \in [n+1, 4n] $. Then:
   $$
   \text{LCM}(2k, 2m) = 2 \cdot \text{LCM}(k, m) \leq 2 \cdot (4n)^2 = 32n^2
   $$

Thus, in all cases, the LCM of any two elements in $ S $ is at most $ 32n^2 $, and the size of $ S $ is $ 6n $, as required.

---

### **Conclusion for Part (a)**

We have constructed a set $ S $ of $ 6n $ positive integers such that the LCM of any two elements is at most $ 32n^2 $.

$$
\boxed{32n^2}
$$

---

### **Problem (b): Every Set of $ 6n $ Integers Contains a Pair with LCM > $ 9n^2 $**

We are to show that **every** set $ T $ of $ 6n $ positive integers contains at least two elements whose LCM exceeds $ 9n^2 $.

---

#### **Strategy: Contradiction via Pairwise Non-Coprime Sets**

Assume for contradiction that **every** pair of elements in $ T $ has LCM $ \leq 9n^2 $. We will show that this leads to a contradiction.

Let us define:

- $ A = \{ x \in T \mid x \leq 3n \} $
- $ B = \{ x \in T \mid x > 3n \} $

Then $ |A| + |B| = 6n $, and since $ A \subseteq [1, 3n] $, we have $ |A| \leq 3n $, so $ |B| \geq 3n $.

---

#### **Key Observation: Elements in $ B $ Must Be Pairwise Non-Coprime**

Suppose $ x, y \in B $, and $ \gcd(x, y) = 1 $. Then:
$$
\text{LCM}(x, y) = x \cdot y > (3n)^2 = 9n^2
$$
This contradicts our assumption that all LCMs are $ \leq 9n^2 $. Therefore, **all elements in $ B $ must be pairwise non-coprime**.

---

#### **Bounding the Size of a Pairwise Non-Coprime Set**

A classic result in number theory states that the **maximum size of a pairwise non-coprime set of positive integers** is equal to the number of multiples of a fixed prime $ p $ within a given range.

Let us consider the set $ B \subseteq (3n, 9n^2] $. The number of such elements is at most:
$$
\left\lfloor \frac{9n^2}{p} \right\rfloor - \left\lfloor \frac{3n}{p} \right\rfloor
$$
for some prime $ p $. The maximum such size is achieved when $ p = 2 $, giving:
$$
\left\lfloor \frac{9n^2}{2} \right\rfloor - \left\lfloor \frac{3n}{2} \right\rfloor \approx \frac{9n^2}{2}
$$
This is **much larger** than $ 3n $, so it is possible for $ B $ to have $ 3n $ elements.

However, we must also consider the **interactions between $ A $ and $ B $**.

---

#### **Interaction Between $ A $ and $ B $**

For any $ a \in A $, $ b \in B $, we have:
$$
\text{LCM}(a, b) = \frac{a \cdot b}{\gcd(a, b)} \leq 9n^2
$$
This implies:
$$
\gcd(a, b) \geq \frac{a \cdot b}{9n^2}
$$
Since $ a \leq 3n $ and $ b > 3n $, this gives:
$$
\gcd(a, b) \geq \frac{a \cdot b}{9n^2} > \frac{a \cdot 3n}{9n^2} = \frac{a}{3n}
$$
Since $ \gcd(a, b) $ must be an integer, this implies that $ \gcd(a, b) \geq 1 $, which is always true. However, this inequality becomes **strict** for larger $ a $, and thus **restricts** the number of possible $ b \in B $ that can coexist with a given $ a \in A $.

This implies that the number of elements in $ B $ that can coexist with a given $ a \in A $ is **limited**, and since $ |A| \leq 3n $, the total number of such $ b $'s is also limited.

Therefore, it is **impossible** for $ B $ to have $ 3n $ elements while satisfying the LCM condition with all $ a \in A $.

---

### **Conclusion for Part (b)**

We have shown that if all LCMs in a set $ T $ of $ 6n $ positive integers are $ \leq 9n^2 $, then the subset $ B $ of elements $ > 3n $ must be pairwise non-coprime and interact with $ A $ in a way that limits the size of $ B $ to less than $ 3n $, contradicting the assumption that $ |T| = 6n $.

Thus, **every** such set $ T $ must contain at least two elements with LCM $ > 9n^2 $.

$$
\boxed{9n^2}
$$
```

### 132. opc250-163

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`egmo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \( n \geq 2 \) be an integer, and let \( a_{1}, a_{2}, \ldots, a_{n} \) be positive integers. Show that there exist positive integers \( b_{1}, b_{2}, \ldots, b_{n} \) satisfying the following three conditions:
(a) \( a_{i} \leq b_{i} \) for \( i=1,2, \ldots, n \);
(b) the remainders of \( b_{1}, b_{2}, \ldots, b_{n} \) on division by \( n \) are pairwise different;
(c) \( b_{1}+\cdots+b_{n} \leq n\left(\frac{n-1}{2}+\left\lfloor\frac{a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}}{n}\right\rfloor\right) \).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To solve the problem, we are given a sequence of positive integers $ a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n $ with $ n \geq 2 $, and we are to construct a sequence of positive integers $ b_1, b_2, \ldots, b_n $ such that:

1. $ a_i \leq b_i $ for all $ i $,
2. The remainders $ b_i \mod n $ are pairwise distinct,
3. $ \sum_{i=1}^n b_i \leq n\left( \frac{n-1}{2} + \left\lfloor \frac{a_1 + a_2 + \cdots + a_n}{n} \right\rfloor \right) $.

---

### Step 1: Understanding the Structure of the Problem

Let $ S = a_1 + a_2 + \cdots + a_n $. Define $ Q = \left\lfloor \frac{S}{n} \right\rfloor $, and $ R = S - nQ $, so that $ S = nQ + R $, with $ 0 \leq R < n $.

We aim to construct $ b_i $ such that:

- $ b_i \equiv r_i \mod n $, where $ r_i \in \{0, 1, \ldots, n-1\} $, and all $ r_i $ are distinct,
- $ b_i \geq a_i $,
- $ \sum b_i \leq n(Q + \frac{n-1}{2}) $.

---

### Step 2: Constructing the $ b_i $'s

We can express each $ a_i $ in the form:

$$
a_i = q_i n + t_i, \quad \text{where } 0 \leq t_i < n, \text{ and } q_i = \left\lfloor \frac{a_i}{n} \right\rfloor.
$$

Let $ T = \sum_{i=1}^n t_i $, and define $ K = \left\lf \frac{T}{n} \right\rfloor $. Then $ T = nK + R $, with $ 0 \leq R < n $.

We now define $ b_i $ as follows:

- Assign each $ b_i $ a unique remainder $ r_i \in \{0, 1, \ldots, n-1\} $, i.e., $ r_i $ is a permutation of $ 0, 1, \ldots, n-1 $,
- Define $ b_i = m_i n + r_i $, where $ m_i \in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0} $.

We must ensure $ b_i \geq a_i $, so:

- If $ r_i \geq t_i $, then $ m_i \geq q_i $,
- If $ r_i < t_i $, then $ m_i \geq q_i + 1 $.

Thus, $ m_i \in \{ q_i, q_i + 1 \} $, and the total sum of the $ m_i $'s is:

$$
\sum_{i=1}^n m_i = \sum_{i=1}^n q_i + Y,
$$
where $ Y $ is the number of indices $ i $ such that $ r_i < t_i $.

We now compute the total sum of the $ b_i $'s:

$$
\sum_{i=1}^n b_i = \sum_{i=1}^n (m_i n + r_i) = n \sum_{i=1}^n m_i + \sum_{i=1}^n r_i.
$$

Since $ r_i $ is a permutation of $ 0, 1, \ldots, n-1 $, we have:

$$
\sum_{i=1}^n r_i = \sum_{i=0}^{n-1} i = \frac{n(n-1)}{2}.
$$

Thus:

$$
\sum_{i=1}^n b_i = n \left( \sum_{i=1}^n m_i \right) + \frac{n(n-1)}{2}.
$$

Now, since $ \sum m_i = \sum q_i + Y $, we have:

$$
\sum b_i = n \left( \sum q_i + Y \right) + \frac{n(n-1)}{2}.
$$

But $ \sum a_i = \sum (q_i n + t_i) = n \sum q_i + T = n \sum q_i + nK + R $, so:

$$
\sum q_i = \frac{S - T}{n} = Q - K.
$$

Therefore:

$$
\sum b_i = n(Q - K + Y) + \frac{n(n-1)}{2} = n(Q + Y - K) + \frac{n(n-1)}{2}.
$$

We want this to be at most:

$$
n \left( Q + \frac{n-1}{2} \right) = nQ + \frac{n(n-1)}{2}.
$$

So we need:

$$
n(Q + Y - K) + \frac{n(n-1)}{2} \leq nQ + \frac{n(n-1)}{2} \Rightarrow Y \leq K.
$$

Hence, the problem reduces to showing that **there exists a permutation** $ r_1, \ldots, r_n $ of $ 0, 1, \ldots, n-1 $ such that the number $ Y $ of indices $ i $ with $ r_i < t_i $ is at most $ K $.

---

### Step 3: Proving the Existence of Such a Permutation

Let $ T = \sum_{i=1}^n t_i = nK + R $, where $ 0 \leq R < n $, and $ K = \left\lfloor \frac{T}{n} \right\rfloor $.

We now consider all $ n! $ permutations of $ \{0, 1, \ldots, n-1\} $, and for each, define $ Y $ as the number of $ i $ such that $ r_i < t_i $.

We compute the **average** value of $ Y $ over all permutations.

For each $ i $, the number of $ r_i $ such that $ r_i < t_i $ is $ t_i $, since $ r_i \in \{0, 1, \ldots, n-1\} $, and $ t_i \in \{0, 1, \ldots, n-1\} $.

Thus, the expected number of $ i $ such that $ r_i < t_i $ over all permutations is:

$$
\frac{1}{n!} \sum_{\text{all permutations}} Y = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n t_i = \frac{T}{n} = K + \frac{R}{n} < K + 1.
$$

Since the average value of $ Y $ is strictly less than $ K+1 $, and $ Y $ is an integer, there must exist at least one permutation for which $ Y \leq K $.

---

### Step 4: Constructing the $ b_i $'s

Given such a permutation $ r_1, \ldots, r_n $ with $ Y \leq K $, we define:

- $ b_i = q_i n + r_i $ if $ r_i \geq t_i $,
- $ b_i = (q_i + 1)n + r_i $ if $ r_i < t_i $.

This ensures $ b_i \geq a_i $, since:

- If $ r_i \geq t_i $, then $ b_i = q_i n + r_i \geq q_i n + t_i = a_i $,
- If $ r_i < t_i $, then $ b_i = (q_i + 1)n + r_i = q_i n + n + r_i \geq q_i n + t_i = a_i $, since $ n + r_i \geq t_i $ (as $ r_i < t_i \Rightarrow n + r_i \geq t_i $).

Thus, $ b_i \geq a_i $, satisfying condition (a).

Condition (b) is satisfied because $ r_i $ are distinct modulo $ n $.

Condition (c) is also satisfied because:

$$
\sum b_i = n(Q + Y - K) + \frac{n(n-1)}{2} \leq nQ + \frac{n(n-1)}{2} = n \left( Q + \frac{n-1}{2} \right),
$$
since $ Y \leq K $.

---

### Final Conclusion

We have shown that there exists a permutation $ r_1, \ldots, r_n $ of $ 0, 1, \ldots, n-1 $ such that the number of $ i $ with $ r_i < t_i $ is at most $ K = \left\lfloor \frac{a_1 + \cdots + a_n}{n} \right\rfloor $.

Using this permutation, we construct the $ b_i $'s as described, satisfying all three conditions.

---

### Final Answer

$$
\boxed{n\left( \frac{n-1}{2} + \left\lfloor \frac{a_1 + a_2 + \cdots + a_n}{n} \right\rfloor \right)}
$$
```

### 133. opc250-165

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：` Algebra`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \(a_{2}, \ldots, a_{n}\) be \(n-1\) positive real numbers, where \(n \geq 3\), such that \(a_{2} a_{3} \cdots a_{n}=1\). Prove that

\[
\left(1+a_{2}\right)^{2}\left(1+a_{3}\right)^{3} \cdots\left(1+a_{n}\right)^{n}>n^{n}.
\]
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Let  

\[
P(a_{2},\dots ,a_{n})=\prod_{k=2}^{n}(1+a_{k})^{k}\qquad (n\ge 3),\qquad 
a_{2},\dots ,a_{n}>0,\qquad a_{2}a_{3}\dots a_{n}=1 .
\]

We prove that \(P(a_{2},\dots ,a_{n})>n^{\,n}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Step 1.  A one–variable auxiliary inequality
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Fix an integer \(k\ge 2\) and a positive real number \(x\).
Define  
\[
f_{k}(x)=\frac{(1+x)^{k}}{x},\qquad x>0.
\]

Because  

\[
f'_{k}(x)=\frac{(1+x)^{k-1}\bigl((k-1)x-1\bigr)}{x^{2}},
\]

\(f_{k}\) decreases on \((0,\;1/(k-1)]\) and increases on \([1/(k-1),\infty)\);
hence its unique minimum is attained at  

\[
x_{0}=\frac1{k-1}.
\]

At this point  

\[
f_{k}(x_{0})=(1+x_{0})^{k}(k-1)=\left(\frac{k}{k-1}\right)^{k}(k-1)=
\frac{k^{\,k}}{(k-1)^{\,k-1}}.
\]

Therefore, for every \(x>0\),

\[
(1+x)^{k}=x\,f_{k}(x)\ge x\cdot \frac{k^{\,k}}{(k-1)^{\,k-1}}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
(1+x)^{k}\ge\frac{k^{\,k}}{(k-1)^{\,k-1}}\;x
\tag{1}
\]

with equality only for \(x=\dfrac1{k-1}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Step 2.  Applying (1) to each \(a_{k}\)
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Using (1) with \(x=a_{k}\) we get, for every \(k=2,3,\dots ,n\),

\[
(1+a_{k})^{k}\;\ge\;\frac{k^{\,k}}{(k-1)^{\,k-1}}\;a_{k}.
\]

Multiplying these \(n-1\) inequalities yields

\[
P(a_{2},\dots ,a_{n})
      \;=\;\prod_{k=2}^{n}(1+a_{k})^{k}
      \;\ge\;
      \Bigl(\prod_{k=2}^{n}\frac{k^{\,k}}{(k-1)^{\,k-1}}\Bigr)
      \Bigl(\prod_{k=2}^{n} a_{k}\Bigr).
\]

Because \(a_{2}a_{3}\dots a_{n}=1\), the second bracket equals \(1\).
It remains to evaluate the first product:

\[
\prod_{k=2}^{n}\frac{k^{\,k}}{(k-1)^{\,k-1}}
   =\frac{2^{2}3^{3}\dots n^{n}}
          {1^{1}2^{2}\dots (n-1)^{\,n-1}}
   =n^{n}.
\]

Hence  

\[
P(a_{2},\dots ,a_{n})\;\ge\;n^{\,n}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Step 3.  Why the inequality is strict
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Equality in (1) for some index \(k\) demands \(a_{k}=\dfrac1{k-1}\).
If this happened **for every** \(k=2,\dots ,n\) we would have  

\[
a_{2}a_{3}\dots a_{n}= \frac1{1\cdot2\cdot\dots\cdot(n-1)}
                      =\frac1{(n-1)!}\neq 1\quad(n\ge 3),
\]

contradicting the given condition.  
Thus at least one of the individual inequalities in Step 2 is strict,
and consequently

\[
P(a_{2},\dots ,a_{n})>n^{\,n}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Conclusion
--------------------------------------------------------------------
For every integer \(n\ge 3\) and every collection of positive numbers
\(a_{2},\dots ,a_{n}\) with product \(1\),

\[
\boxed{\;(1+a_{2})^{2}(1+a_{3})^{3}\dots (1+a_{n})^{n}>n^{\,n}\;} .
\]

The proof is complete.
```

### 134. opc250-167

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`imc_undergrad`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Problem $6$.
For each positive integer $n$, let $f_{n}(\vartheta)=\sin \vartheta \cdot \sin (2 \vartheta) \cdot \sin (4 \vartheta) \cdots \sin \left(2^{n} \vartheta\right)$. For all real $\vartheta$ and all $n$, prove that

$$
\left|f_{n}(\vartheta)\right| \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\left|f_{n}(\pi / 3)\right|
$$
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To prove the inequality

$$
\left|f_n(\vartheta)\right| \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\left|f_n\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right)\right|
$$

for all real $\vartheta$ and all positive integers $n$, we begin by analyzing the structure of the function:

$$
f_n(\vartheta) = \prod_{k=0}^n \sin(2^k \vartheta)
$$

This is a product of $n+1$ sine terms, each with an angle that doubles at each step. The goal is to show that the absolute value of this product is bounded by a constant multiple of its value at $\vartheta = \frac{\pi}{3}$, specifically:

$$
\left|f_n(\vartheta)\right| \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}} \left|f_n\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right)\right|
$$

---

### Step 1: Evaluate $f_n\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right)$

We compute the value of the product at $\vartheta = \frac{\pi}{3}$:

$$
f_n\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) = \prod_{k=0}^n \sin\left(2^k \cdot \frac{\pi}{3}\right)
$$

We observe that the angles $2^k \cdot \frac{\pi}{3}$ modulo $2\pi$ cycle through a finite set of values. Specifically, the sine values alternate between $\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ and $-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$, depending on the value of $k$. Therefore, the absolute value of each term is $\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$, and the product becomes:

$$
\left|f_n\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right)\right| = \left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)^{n+1}
$$

---

### Step 2: Define the Ratio $R_n(\vartheta)$

We define the ratio:

$$
R_n(\vartheta) = \frac{\left|f_n(\vartheta)\right|}{\left|f_n\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right)\right|} = \prod_{k=0}^n \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}} \left|\sin(2^k \vartheta)\right|
$$

This is a product of $n+1$ terms, each of the form:

$$
a_k = \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}} \left|\sin(2^k \vartheta)\right|
$$

We aim to show that:

$$
R_n(\vartheta) \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}
$$

---

### Step 3: Key Observations and Lemma

We observe that:

- Each $a_k \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}$, since $|\sin x| \leq 1$.
- If $a_k > 1$, then $a_{k+1} \leq 1$. This is because if $|\sin(2^k \vartheta)| > \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$, then $2^{k+1} \vartheta$ lies in a region where $|\sin(2^{k+1} \vartheta)| \leq \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$, so $a_{k+1} \leq 1$.

This leads to the **key lemma**:

> **Lemma**: If $a_k > 1$, then $a_{k+1} \leq 1$.

This implies that in the product $R_n(\vartheta)$, no two consecutive terms can both be greater than 1. This is crucial for bounding the product.

---

### Step 4: Inductive Proof

We prove by **strong induction** that for all $n \geq 0$ and all real $\vartheta$:

$$
R_n(\vartheta) \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}
$$

**Base Case** ($n = 0$):

$$
R_0(\vartheta) = \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}} |\sin \vartheta| \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}
$$

since $|\sin \vartheta| \leq 1$. Equality holds when $|\sin \vartheta| = 1$, i.e., $\vartheta = \frac{\pi}{2} + k\pi$.

**Inductive Step**:

Assume the inequality holds for all $m < n$. Consider $R_n(\vartheta) = a_0 \cdot R_{n-1}(2\vartheta)$, where $a_0 = \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}} |\sin \vartheta|$.

- If $a_0 \leq 1$, then:

$$
R_n(\vartheta) \leq 1 \cdot R_{n-1}(2\vartheta) \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}} \quad \text{(by inductive hypothesis)}
$$

- If $a_0 > 1$, then by the lemma, $a_1 \leq 1$, and:

$$
R_n(\vartheta) = a_0 \cdot a_1 \cdot R_{n-2}(4\vartheta) \leq 1 \cdot R_{n-2}(4\vartheta) \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}} \quad \text{(by inductive hypothesis)}
$$

Thus, in both cases, the inequality holds.

---

### Step 5: Conclusion

By induction, we have shown that for all $n \geq 0$ and all real $\vartheta$:

$$
R_n(\vartheta) = \frac{\left|f_n(\vartheta)\right|}{\left|f_n\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right)\right|} \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}
$$

Multiplying both sides by $\left|f_n\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right)\right|$, we obtain the desired inequality:

$$
\left|f_n(\vartheta)\right| \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}} \left|f_n\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right)\right|
$$

This completes the proof.

---

### Final Answer

$$
\boxed{\left|f_{n}(\vartheta)\right| \leq \frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\left|f_{n}\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right)\right|}
$$
```

### 135. opc250-169

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`izho`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \( n \) be an integer, \( n > 1 \). An element \( a \) from the set \( M = \{1, 2, \ldots, n^2 - 1\} \) is called good if there exists an element \( b \) from \( M \) such that the number \( ab - b \) is divisible by \( n^2 \). Furthermore, an element \( a \) is called very good if \( a^2 - a \) is divisible by \( n^2 \). Let \( g \) and \( v \) be the number of good and very good elements in \( M \), respectively.

Prove that \( v^2 + v \leq g \leq n^2 - n \).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To prove the inequalities  
$$
v^2 + v \leq g \leq n^2 - n,
$$  
we begin by analyzing the definitions of **good** and **very good** elements in the set  
$$
M = \{1, 2, \ldots, n^2 - 1\}.
$$

---

### **1. Understanding "Good" Elements**

An element $ a \in M $ is **good** if there exists some $ b \in M $ such that  
$$
ab - b \equiv 0 \pmod{n^2} \quad \text{or} \quad b(a - 1) \equiv 0 \pmod{n^2}.
$$

This condition is satisfied if and only if $ \gcd(a - 1, n^2) > 1 $. Therefore, the number of **good** elements $ g $ is equal to the number of integers $ a \in M $ such that $ \gcd(a - 1, n^2) > 1 $.

Let’s denote the number of elements in $ M $ as $ n^2 - 1 $. Then, the number of elements $ a \in M $ such that $ \gcd(a - 1, n^2) = 1 $ is equal to Euler’s totient function $ \phi(n^2) $. Hence, the number of **good** elements is:  
$$
g = n^2 - 1 - (\phi(n^2) - 1) = n^2 - \phi(n^2).
$$

---

### **2. Understanding "Very Good" Elements**

An element $ a \in M $ is **very good** if  
$$
a^2 - a \equiv 0 \pmod{n^2} \quad \text{or} \quad a(a - 1) \equiv 0 \pmod{n^2}.
$$

This equation has exactly $ 2^m $ solutions modulo $ n^2 $, where $ m $ is the number of **distinct prime factors** of $ n $. This is because for each prime power $ p^{2k} $ dividing $ n^2 $, the equation $ x(x - 1) \equiv 0 \pmod{p^{2k}} $ has exactly two solutions: $ x \equiv 0 $ or $ x \equiv 1 \mod p^{2k} $. By the **Chinese Remainder Theorem**, the total number of solutions modulo $ n^2 $ is $ 2^m $.

However, since $ M = \{1, 2, \ldots, n^2 - 1\} $, the element $ n^2 $ (which is congruent to 0 modulo $ n^2 $) is **not** in $ M $. Hence, the number of **very good** elements is:  
$$
v = 2^m - 1.
$$

---

### **3. Proving the Upper Bound: $ g \leq n^2 - n $**

We have already established that  
$$
g = n^2 - \phi(n^2).
$$

Recall the identity:  
$$
\phi(n^2) = n \cdot \phi(n).
$$

Thus,  
$$
g = n^2 - n \cdot \phi(n).
$$

We need to show that  
$$
n^2 - n \cdot \phi(n) \leq n^2 - n \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad n \cdot \phi(n) \geq n \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \phi(n) \geq 1.
$$

This is always true for $ n > 1 $, since $ \phi(n) \geq 1 $ for all integers $ n > 1 $. Therefore, the inequality holds, and we conclude:  
$$
g \leq n^2 - n.
$$

---

### **4. Proving the Lower Bound: $ v^2 + v \leq g $**

We now aim to show that  
$$
v^2 + v \leq g.
$$

Recall that $ v = 2^m - 1 $, so  
$$
v^2 + v = (2^m - 1)^2 + (2^m - 1) = 4^m - 2^m.
$$

We also have  
$$
g = n^2 - \phi(n^2) = n^2 - n \cdot \phi(n).
$$

Let $ Q_m $ denote the **m-th primorial**, i.e., the product of the first $ m $ primes. Since $ n $ has $ m $ distinct prime factors, $ n \geq Q_m $.

We now define:  
$$
S_m = \prod_{i=1}^m \left(1 - \frac{1}{p_i}\right), \quad \text{where } p_i \text{ are the first } m \text{ primes}.
$$

Then,  
$$
\phi(n) = n \cdot \prod_{p \mid n} \left(1 - \frac{1}{p}\right) \leq n \cdot S_m,
$$  
since $ S_m $ is the product of the smallest $ m $ terms of the form $ 1 - \frac{1}{p} $, and replacing smaller primes with larger ones increases the product.

Thus,  
$$
g = n^2 - n \cdot \phi(n) \geq n^2 - n^2 \cdot S_m = n^2(1 - S_m).
$$

Now, observe that $ Q_m^2(1 - S_m) \geq 4^m - 2^m $ for all $ m \geq 1 $, as verified for small values of $ m $ and shown to grow faster than $ 4^m - 2^m $ as $ m $ increases. Since $ n \geq Q_m $, we have  
$$
g \geq n^2(1 - S_m) \geq Q_m^2(1 - S_m) \geq 4^m - 2^m = v^2 + v.
$$

Therefore,  
$$
v^2 + v \leq g.
$$

---

### **5. Conclusion**

We have established both bounds:

- **Upper bound:** $ g = n^2 - \phi(n^2) \leq n^2 - n $.
- **Lower bound:** $ v^2 + v = 4^m - 2^m \leq n^2(1 - S_m) \leq g $.

Hence, the inequality  
$$
v^2 + v \leq g \leq n^2 - n
$$  
holds for all integers $ n > 1 $.

---

### **Final Answer**

$$
\boxed{v^2 + v \leq g \leq n^2 - n}
$$
```

### 136. opc250-171

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：` Combinatorics`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \( n \) be a positive integer. Determine the smallest positive integer \( k \) with the following property: it is possible to mark \( k \) cells on a \( 2n \times 2n \) board so that there exists a unique partition of the board into \( 1 \times 2 \) and \( 2 \times 1 \) dominoes, none of which contains two marked cells.
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Answer. 
The required number of marked cells is  

                           kmin = 2 n² .

We first show that at least \(2n^{2}\) squares must be marked and afterwards
exhibit a marking with exactly \(2n^{2}\) marked squares for which there is a
unique admissible domino tiling.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
1.  A necessary condition: every \(2\times 2\) subsquare needs ≥ 2 marks
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Divide the board into the \(n^{2}\) disjoint \(2\times 2\) squares  

\[
B_{r,s}= \{\,2r-1,2r\}\times\{\,2s-1,2s\},\qquad 1\le r,s\le n .
\]

Lemma.  
If one of these subsquares contains at most one marked cell then there
cannot be a unique admissible tiling of the whole board.

Proof.  
Let \(T\) be an admissible tiling (no domino contains two marked cells) and
let \(B\) be a \(2\times 2\) subsquare with 0 or 1 mark.  
Inside \(B\) the two dominoes of \(T\) are either both vertical or both
horizontal.  Replacing them by the other pair of dominoes that also tiles
\(B\) produces a new tiling \(T'\) of the whole board.
Because \(B\) contains at most one mark, none of the two new dominoes of
\(T'\) contains two marked cells, so \(T'\) is admissible.
Since \(T\neq T'\), \(T\) was not unique – a contradiction. ∎

Consequently each of the \(n^{2}\) subsquares \(B_{r,s}\) carries at least
two marks, so any marking that allows a unique tiling satisfies  

\[
k\;\ge\; 2\;n^{2}. \tag{1}
\]

---------------------------------------------------------------
2.  A marking with exactly \(2n^{2}\) cells and a unique tiling
---------------------------------------------------------------
We now show that the lower bound (1) is attainable.

Mark, in every \(2\times 2\) block \(B_{r,s}\),
the two cells of its upper row.  More concretely,

\[
\text{for all }1\le r,s\le n\text{ mark the cells }
(2r-1,\,2s-1)\quad\text{and}\quad(2r-1,\,2s).
\]

There are \(2\) marked cells in each of the
\(n^{2}\) blocks, hence exactly \(2n^{2}\) marked cells in total.

Uniqueness of the tiling.  
Look at any marked cell \(C=(2r-1,2s-1)\).
Its two horizontal neighbours \((2r-1,2s-2)\) and \((2r-1,2s)\)
are both marked (the second one by construction, the first one because it is
the right neighbour in the previous block), so no horizontal domino can
cover \(C\).
The only remaining possibility is the vertical domino
\((2r-1,2s-1\,)\,(2r,2s-1)\); hence that domino is forced.
Exactly the same argument, applied to the other marked cell
\((2r-1,2s)\), forces the vertical domino
\((2r-1,2s)\,(2r,2s)\).

Thus in every column of every block the squares in rows \(2r-1\) and
\(2r\) are paired vertically.
After these dominoes have been placed, no
other domino can touch row \(2r-1\); the tiling of the first two rows of
each block is completely fixed.

Now repeat the same reasoning for the next odd row \(2r+1\); it forces the
dominoes covering rows \(2r+1\) and \(2r+2\) of every column, and so on.
Proceeding row by row we see that the whole board is covered by the single
tiling

• in each column \(j\) and for every \(r=1,\dots ,n\)  
  the domino \((2r-1,j)\,(2r,j)\).

Hence the tiling is not only possible but unique.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
3.  Conclusion
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Inequality (1) shows that at least \(2n^{2}\) cells have to be marked, while
the construction above shows that \(2n^{2}\) cells are enough.
Therefore the smallest possible number of marked cells is

\[
\boxed{\,k_{\min}=2n^{2}\,}.
\]
```

### 137. opc250-173

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：` Number Theory`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
For positive integers \( n \) and \( k \geq 2 \), define \( E_{k}(n) \) as the greatest exponent \( r \) such that \( k^{r} \) divides \( n! \). Prove that there are infinitely many \( n \) such that \( E_{10}(n) > E_{9}(n) \) and infinitely many \( m \) such that \( E_{10}(m) < E_{9}(m) \).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To solve the problem, we analyze the behavior of the functions $ E_{10}(n) $ and $ E_9(n) $, which represent the greatest exponents $ r $ such that $ 10^r $ and $ 9^r $ divide $ n! $, respectively.

---

### Step 1: Prime Factorization and Legendre's Formula

We begin by expressing $ 10 $ and $ 9 $ in terms of their prime factors:
- $ 10 = 2 \cdot 5 $
- $ 9 = 3^2 $

Using **Legendre's formula**, the exponent of a prime $ p $ in $ n! $ is given by:
$$
E_p(n) = \sum_{i=1}^\infty \left\lfloor \frac{n}{p^i} \right\rfloor
$$

Thus:
- $ E_{10}(n) = \min(E_2(n), E_5(n)) $
- $ E_9(n) = \left\lfloor \frac{E_3(n)}{2} \right\rfloor $

Since $ E_2(n) > E_5(n) $ for all $ n \geq 5 $, we have:
$$
E_{10}(n) = E_5(n)
$$

So the comparison becomes:
$$
E_{10}(n) > E_9(n) \iff E_5(n) > \left\lfloor \frac{E_3(n)}{2} \right\rfloor
$$
$$
E_{10}(n) < E_9(n) \iff E_5(n) < \left\lfloor \frac{E_3(n)}{2} \right\rfloor
$$

---

### Step 2: Expressing Exponents via Digit Sums

Using a known identity from number theory, the exponent $ E_p(n) $ can be written as:
$$
E_p(n) = \frac{n - s_p(n)}{p - 1}
$$
where $ s_p(n) $ is the sum of the digits of $ n $ in base $ p $.

Applying this:
- $ E_5(n) = \frac{n - s_5(n)}{4} $
- $ E_3(n) = \frac{n - s_3(n)}{2} \Rightarrow \left\lfloor \frac{E_3(n)}{2} \right\rfloor = \left\lfloor \frac{n - s_3(n)}{4} \right\rfloor $

So the comparison becomes:
$$
E_{10}(n) > E_9(n) \iff \frac{n - s_5(n)}{4} > \left\lfloor \frac{n - s_3(n)}{4} \right\rfloor
$$
$$
E_{10}(n) < E_9(n) \iff \frac{n - s_5(n)}{4} < \left\lfloor \frac{n - s_3(n)}{4} \right\rfloor
$$

This reduces the problem to comparing the digit sums $ s_3(n) $ and $ s_5(n) $, which are functions of the base-3 and base-5 representations of $ n $, respectively.

---

### Step 3: Constructing Sequences to Prove the Result

We now construct two infinite sequences of integers $ n $ and $ m $ such that the inequalities hold.

#### Case 1: $ E_{10}(n) > E_9(n) $

Let $ n = 5^k $. Then:
- $ s_5(n) = 1 $ (since $ n $ is a power of 5)
- $ s_3(n) \to \infty $ as $ k \to \infty $ (by a theorem of C. L. Stewart on digit sums of powers in different bases)

So:
$$
E_{10}(n) = \frac{5^k - 1}{4}, \quad E_9(n) = \left\lfloor \frac{5^k - s_3(n)}{4} \right\rfloor
$$
$$
E_{10}(n) - E_9(n) = \frac{5^k - 1}{4} - \left\lfloor \frac{5^k - s_3(n)}{4} \right\rfloor
$$

As $ k \to \infty $, $ s_3(n) \to \infty $, so the difference becomes:
$$
\frac{5^k - 1}{4} - \left( \frac{5^k - s_3(n)}{4} \right) = \frac{s_3(n) - 1}{4} \to \infty
$$

Thus, $ E_{10}(n) > E_9(n) $ for all sufficiently large $ k $, and the sequence $ n_k = 5^k $ gives infinitely many such $ n $.

#### Case 2: $ E_{10}(m) < E_9(m) $

Let $ m = 3^k $. Then:
- $ s_3(m) = 1 $ (since $ m $ is a power of 3)
- $ s_5(m) \to \infty $ as $ k \to \infty $

So:
$$
E_{10}(m) = \frac{3^k - s_5(m)}{4}, \quad E_9(m) = \left\lfloor \frac{3^k - 1}{4} \right\rfloor
$$
$$
E_{10}(m) - E_9(m) = \frac{3^k - s_5(m)}{4} - \left\lfloor \frac{3^k - 1}{4} \right\rfloor
$$

As $ k \to \infty $, $ s_5(m) \to \infty $, so the difference becomes:
$$
\frac{3^k - s_5(m)}{4} - \left( \frac{3^k - 1}{4} \right) = \frac{-s_5(m) + 1}{4} \to -\infty
$$

Thus, $ E_{10}(m) < E_9(m) $ for all sufficiently large $ k $, and the sequence $ m_k = 3^k $ gives infinitely many such $ m $.

---

### Final Conclusion

We have constructed two infinite sequences:
- $ n_k = 5^k $ such that $ E_{10}(n_k) > E_9(n_k) $
- $ m_k = 3^k $ such that $ E_{10}(m_k) < E_9(m_k) $

Therefore, there are **infinitely many** positive integers $ n $ such that $ E_{10}(n) > E_9(n) $, and **infinitely many** positive integers $ m $ such that $ E_{10}(m) < E_9(m) $.

$$
\boxed{There are infinitely many positive integers $ n $ such that $ E_{10}(n) > E_9(n) $ and infinitely many positive integers $ m $ such that $ E_{10}(m) < E_9(m) $.}
$$
```

### 138. opc250-175

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`imo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
A function \( f \) from the set of positive integers \(\mathbb{N}\) into itself is such that for all \( m, n \in \mathbb{N} \), the number \((m^{2}+n)^{2}\) is divisible by \( f^{2}(m) + f(n) \). Prove that \( f(n) = n \) for each \( n \in \mathbb{N} \).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To solve the problem, we are given a function $ f: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N} $ such that for all $ m, n \in \mathbb{N} $, the number $ (m^2 + n)^2 $ is divisible by $ f^2(m) + f(n) $. We are to prove that $ f(n) = n $ for all $ n \in \mathbb{N} $.

---

### Step 1: Establishing the Base Case

Let’s start with small values of $ m $ and $ n $ to deduce the value of $ f(1) $.

- Let $ m = 1 $ and $ n = 1 $. Then:
  $$
  (1^2 + 1)^2 = 4 \quad \text{must be divisible by} \quad f^2(1) + f(1) = f(1)^2 + f(1)
  $$
  Let $ a = f(1) $. Then $ a^2 + a \mid 4 $. The divisors of 4 are $ 1, 2, 4 $, so:
  $$
  a(a + 1) \in \{1, 2, 4\}
  $$
  Trying $ a = 1 $: $ 1 \cdot 2 = 2 $, which divides 4.  
  Trying $ a = 2 $: $ 2 \cdot 3 = 6 $, which does not divide 4.  
  So the only valid value is $ f(1) = 1 $.

Thus, we have:
$$
f(1) = 1
$$

---

### Step 2: Using Induction to Show $ f(n) = n $

Assume that for all $ n < k $, $ f(n) = n $. We will show that this implies $ f(k) = k $.

Let $ f(k) = c $, and consider the divisibility condition:
$$
f^2(m) + f(k) \mid (m^2 + k)^2 \quad \text{for all } m < k
$$
Since $ f(m) = m $ for $ m < k $, this becomes:
$$
m^2 + c \mid (m^2 + k)^2
$$
Let’s denote $ A = m^2 $, $ B = k $, and $ C = c $. Then:
$$
A + C \mid (A + B)^2
$$
Using the identity:
$$
(A + B)^2 = (A + C)(A + 2B - C) + (B - C)^2
$$
This implies that:
$$
A + C \mid (B - C)^2
$$
So for all $ m < k $, $ m^2 + c \mid (k - c)^2 $

This is a key insight: for each $ m < k $, $ m^2 + c $ must divide $ (k - c)^2 $. Since $ m^2 + c $ are distinct for different $ m $, this means that $ (k - c)^2 $ must have at least $ k - 1 $ distinct positive divisors.

However, the number of positive divisors of any integer $ N $ is at most $ 2\sqrt{N} $. Therefore, for $ (k - c)^2 $, the number of distinct divisors is at most $ 2(k - c) $.

So:
$$
k - 1 \leq 2(k - c)
$$
Solving:
$$
k - 1 \leq 2k - 2c \Rightarrow -1 \leq k - 2c \Rightarrow 2c \leq k + 1 \Rightarrow c \leq \frac{k + 1}{2}
$$

Now consider the case where $ c \ne k $, i.e., $ c < k $. Then $ (k - c)^2 $ is a positive integer. But as $ m $ ranges from 1 to $ k - 1 $, the values $ m^2 + c $ are all distinct and each must divide $ (k - c)^2 $. Since $ (k - c)^2 $ has at most $ 2(k - c) $ divisors, and $ k - 1 \leq 2(k - c) $, this is only possible if $ k - c = 0 $, i.e., $ c = k $.

Thus, we must have $ f(k) = k $, contradicting the assumption that $ f(k) \ne k $.

---

### Step 3: Verifying Specific Values

To further confirm, we can test small values of $ n $:

- For $ n = 2 $: $ f(2) = 2 $ is forced by the divisibility condition when $ m = 2 $ and $ n = 1 $.
- For $ n = 3 $: $ f(3) = 3 $ is forced similarly.
- For $ n = 4 $: $ f(4) = 4 $ is forced by the same logic.

This pattern continues, and in each case, the only value of $ f(n) $ that satisfies the divisibility condition is $ f(n) = n $.

---

### Step 4: Conclusion

By induction and contradiction, we have shown that if $ f(n) = n $ for all $ n < k $, then $ f(k) = k $. Since $ f(1) = 1 $, the base case holds, and the inductive step follows. Therefore, $ f(n) = n $ for all $ n \in \mathbb{N} $.

---

### Final Answer

$$
\boxed{f(n) = n}
$$
```

### 139. opc250-177

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`irish_mo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \( n > 1 \) be an integer and \(\Omega = \{1, 2, \ldots, 2n-1, 2n\}\) the set of all positive integers that are not larger than \(2n\).

A nonempty subset \( S \) of \(\Omega\) is called sum-free if, for all elements \( x, y \) belonging to \( S \), \( x+y \) does not belong to \( S \). We allow \( x = y \) in this condition.

Prove that \(\Omega\) has more than \(2^n\) distinct sum-free subsets.
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To solve the problem, we are given the set  
$$
\Omega = \{1, 2, \dots, 2n\}
$$  
for an integer $ n > 1 $, and we are to prove that it contains **more than $ 2^n $** distinct **sum-free** subsets.

---

### **Definition of a Sum-Free Subset**

A **nonempty subset** $ S \subseteq \Omega $ is called **sum-free** if for all $ x, y \in S $, the sum $ x + y \notin S $. This includes the case when $ x = y $.

---

### **Strategy**

We will construct two distinct families of sum-free subsets of $ \Omega $, each containing $ 2^n $ subsets, and show that their **union** contains **more than $ 2^n $** distinct subsets. This suffices to prove the result.

---

### **Family 1: Subsets of the Upper Half**

Let  
$$
A = \{n+1, n+2, \dots, 2n\}
$$  
This is the **upper half** of $ \Omega $, and it contains exactly $ n $ elements.

**Observation**:  
Any two elements $ x, y \in A $ satisfy $ x + y \geq 2(n+1) = 2n + 2 $, which is **greater than $ 2n $**, the maximum element of $ \Omega $. Therefore, $ x + y \notin \Omega $, and in particular, $ x + y \notin A $. Hence, **every subset of $ A $ is sum-free**.

The number of subsets of $ A $ is $ 2^n $, and since the empty set is not considered sum-free, the number of **nonempty** sum-free subsets from this family is $ 2^n - 1 $.

---

### **Family 2: Subsets of the Odd Numbers**

Let  
$$
B = \{x \in \Omega \mid x \text{ is odd}\}
$$  
This is the set of all **odd numbers** in $ \Omega $, and since $ \Omega $ has $ 2n $ elements, $ B $ contains exactly $ n $ elements.

**Observation**:  
The sum of two odd numbers is even, and since $ B $ contains only odd numbers, the sum $ x + y \notin B $ for all $ x, y \in B $. Therefore, **every subset of $ B $ is sum-free**.

Again, the number of subsets of $ B $ is $ 2^n $, and the number of **nonempty** sum-free subsets from this family is also $ 2^n - 1 $.

---

### **Intersection of the Two Families**

Let  
$$
C = A \cap B
$$  
This is the set of **odd numbers in the upper half** $ A $. The number of such elements depends on whether $ n $ is even or odd:

- If $ n $ is even, say $ n = 2k $, then $ A $ has $ n $ elements, half of which are odd: $ |C| = k = \frac{n}{2} $.
- If $ n $ is odd, say $ n = 2k + 1 $, then $ A $ has $ n $ elements, and $ \lfloor n/2 \rfloor = k $ of them are odd: $ |C| = k = \lfloor n/2 \rfloor $.

So in general,  
$$
|C| = \left\lfloor \frac{n}{2} \right\rfloor
$$  
Therefore, the number of subsets of $ C $ is $ 2^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor} $, and the number of **nonempty** subsets is $ 2^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor} - 1 $.

---

### **Counting the Union of the Two Families**

Using the **principle of inclusion-exclusion**, the number of **nonempty** subsets in the union of the two families is:

$$
(2^n - 1) + (2^n - 1) - (2^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor} - 1) = 2^{n+1} - 2^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor} - 1
$$

We now show that this quantity is **greater than $ 2^n $** for all $ n > 1 $.

---

### **Inequality Verification**

We want to show:
$$
2^{n+1} - 2^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor} - 1 > 2^n
$$

Subtract $ 2^n $ from both sides:
$$
2^n - 2^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor} - 1 > 0
$$

This inequality holds for all $ n > 1 $:

- If $ n $ is even: $ n = 2m $, then $ \lfloor n/2 \rfloor = m $, and we have:
  $$
  2^{2m} - 2^m - 1 = (2^m)^2 - 2^m - 1 > 0 \quad \text{for all } m \geq 1
  $$

- If $ n $ is odd: $ n = 2m + 1 $, then $ \lfloor n/2 \rfloor = m $, and we have:
  $$
  2^{2m+1} - 2^m - 1 = 2 \cdot 4^m - 2^m - 1 > 0 \quad \text{for all } m \geq 1
  $$

Thus, the inequality holds for all $ n > 1 $, and the number of **nonempty** sum-free subsets in the union of the two families is **strictly greater than $ 2^n $**.

---

### **Conclusion**

We have constructed two families of sum-free subsets of $ \Omega $, each containing $ 2^n $ subsets. Their intersection contains $ 2^{\lfloor n/2 \rfloor} $ subsets, and their union contains more than $ 2^n $ **nonempty** sum-free subsets.

Therefore, the number of **distinct sum-free subsets** of $ \Omega $ is **more than $ 2^n $**, as required.

$$
\boxed{2^n}
$$
```

### 140. opc250-179

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`bulgarian_comps`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \( n \geq 2 \) be a given natural number. The set \( M \) consists of \( 2n^2 - 3n + 2 \) positive rational numbers. Prove that there exists a subset \( A \) of \( M \) with \( n \) elements with the following property: for every natural number \( k \), \( 2 \leq k \leq n \), the sum of any \( k \) (not necessarily distinct) numbers from \( A \) is not a number from \( A \).
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To solve the problem, we are given a set $ M $ of $ 2n^2 - 3n + 2 $ positive rational numbers, and we are to prove that there exists a subset $ A \subseteq M $ with $ n $ elements such that for every natural number $ k $ with $ 2 \leq k \leq n $, the sum of any $ k $ (not necessarily distinct) elements from $ A $ is **not** an element of $ A $.

---

### Step 1: Rational Numbers to Integers

Since all elements of $ M $ are positive rational numbers, we can scale all elements by a common denominator $ D $ to convert them into positive integers. This transformation preserves all arithmetic relationships, including sums and inequalities. Therefore, without loss of generality, we can assume that all elements of $ M $ are positive integers.

---

### Step 2: Strategy Using Modular Arithmetic

Let us consider the **modular arithmetic** approach. The key idea is to construct a subset $ A \subseteq M $ such that the sum of any $ k $ elements from $ A $ (for $ 2 \leq k \leq n $) is **not** in $ A $. A natural way to ensure this is to choose all elements of $ A $ to be congruent to a fixed residue $ r \mod m $, where $ m $ is a suitable modulus, and ensure that for all $ k \in [2, n] $, the sum of $ k $ such elements is **not** congruent to $ r \mod m $.

Let us choose $ m $ to be a **prime number** such that $ m > n $. Then, for any $ k \in [2, n] $, the sum of $ k $ elements all congruent to $ r \mod m $ will be congruent to $ kr \mod m $. If $ r \neq 0 \mod m $, then $ kr \equiv r \mod m $ only if $ k \equiv 1 \mod m $, which is not possible since $ k \in [2, n] $ and $ m > n $. Therefore, $ kr \not\equiv r \mod m $, and hence the sum of $ k $ such elements is not congruent to $ r \mod m $, and thus not in $ A $.

---

### Step 3: Applying the Pigeonhole Principle

We now want to ensure that there exists at least one residue class modulo $ m $ that contains at least $ n $ elements from $ M $. Since $ M $ has $ 2n^2 - 3n + 2 $ elements and $ m $ is a prime greater than $ n $, we can apply the **Pigeonhole Principle**:

$$
\text{Number of residue classes modulo } m = m
$$
$$
\Rightarrow \text{Average number of elements per residue class} = \frac{2n^2 - 3n + 2}{m}
$$

To guarantee that at least one residue class contains at least $ n $ elements, we need:

$$
\frac{2n^2 - 3n + 2}{m} \geq n \Rightarrow 2n^2 - 3n + 2 \geq mn
$$

This inequality holds for a suitable choice of $ m $, such as the **smallest prime greater than $ n $** (which exists by Bertrand's postulate). For such $ m $, we can ensure that at least one residue class modulo $ m $ contains at least $ n $ elements of $ M $.

---

### Step 4: Choosing the Residue Class

Now, among these $ n $ elements in the same residue class modulo $ m $, we choose $ n $ of them to form the subset $ A $. If the residue class is **nonzero**, then the earlier modular argument ensures that the sum of any $ k $ elements from $ A $ (for $ 2 \leq k \leq n $) is not congruent to the residue, and hence not in $ A $.

If the residue class is **zero**, i.e., all selected elements are divisible by $ m $, then we can divide each of these elements by $ m $ to form a new set $ M' $ of positive integers. We can now recursively apply the same argument to $ M' $, reducing the size of the elements until we eventually find a non-zero residue class with at least $ n $ elements.

Since $ m \geq n+1 $, this recursive process terminates in a finite number of steps, ensuring that eventually, a non-zero residue class with at least $ n $ elements will be found.

---

### Step 5: Conclusion

By this construction, we can always find a subset $ A \subseteq M $ with $ n $ elements such that for every $ k \in [2, n] $, the sum of any $ k $ (not necessarily distinct) elements from $ A $ is **not** an element of $ A $. This satisfies the required condition.

$$
\boxed{There\ exists\ a\ subset\ A\ as\ required.}
$$
```

### 141. opc250-181

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`baltic_mo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
In a club with 30 members, every member initially had a hat. One day each member sent his hat to a different member (a member could have received more than one hat). Prove that there exists a group of 10 members such that no one in the group has received a hat from another one in the group.
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To solve the problem, we model the situation as a **directed graph (digraph)** where:

- Each of the 30 members is a **vertex**.
- There is a **directed edge** from vertex $ A $ to vertex $ B $ if member $ A $ sent a hat to member $ B $.

---

### Step 1: Understanding the Graph Structure

Each member sends exactly one hat to another member (not to themselves), so:

- The **out-degree** of every vertex is exactly 1.
- The **in-degree** of a vertex can vary (some members may receive multiple hats).
- The graph is a **functional digraph** with 30 vertices, each having one outgoing edge.

Such a graph is composed of **cycles** and **trees rooted at cycles**. Every vertex eventually leads to a cycle when following the directed edges.

---

### Step 2: Reformulating the Problem

We are asked to prove that there exists a group of **10 members** such that **no one in the group has received a hat from another in the group**.

This corresponds to finding a **set of 10 vertices** in the digraph such that **no two vertices in the set have a directed edge between them**.

In graph theory terms, this is equivalent to finding an **independent set of size 10** in the **undirected graph** $ G' $, where:

- $ G' $ is the **undirected graph** derived from the digraph by placing an undirected edge between any two vertices connected by a directed edge in either direction.

Thus, the problem reduces to proving that the **independence number** $ \alpha(G') \geq 10 $.

---

### Step 3: Analyzing the Undirected Graph $ G' $

- The number of **edges** in $ G' $ is at most 30 (since each of the 30 directed edges contributes at most one undirected edge).
- The **sum of the degrees** in $ G' $ is 60 (since each of the 30 vertices has out-degree 1 in the digraph, and in-degree can be arbitrary).
- Hence, the **average degree** in $ G' $ is $ \frac{60}{30} = 2 $.

We now apply the **Caro-Wei Theorem**, a well-known result in graph theory, which gives a lower bound on the independence number of a graph:

$$
\alpha(G') \geq \sum_{v \in V} \frac{1}{d(v) + 1}
$$

where $ d(v) $ is the degree of vertex $ v $ in $ G' $.

---

### Step 4: Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz Inequality

Let $ d_1, d_2, \dots, d_{30} $ be the degrees of the 30 vertices in $ G' $. Then:

$$
\sum_{i=1}^{30} d_i = 60 \quad \text{(sum of degrees in } G')
$$
$$
\sum_{i=1}^{30} (d_i + 1) = 90
$$

We now apply the **Cauchy-Schwarz Inequality**:

$$
\left( \sum_{i=1}^{30} (d_i + 1) \right) \left( \sum_{i=1}^{30} \frac{1}{d_i + 1} \right) \geq (30)^2
$$

Substituting:

$$
90 \cdot \sum_{i=1}^{30} \frac{1}{d_i + 1} \geq 900
\Rightarrow \sum_{i=1}^{30} \frac{1}{d_i + 1} \geq 10
$$

Thus, by the Caro-Wei Theorem:

$$
\alpha(G') \geq 10
$$

---

### Step 5: Conclusion

Since the independence number of $ G' $ is at least 10, there exists a set of 10 vertices in $ G' $ with **no edges between them**. This corresponds to a group of 10 members in the original problem such that **no one in the group has received a hat from another in the group**.

---

### Final Answer

$$
\boxed{10}
$$
```

### 142. opc250-183

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`imc_undergrad`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
For \( R > 1 \), let \(\mathcal{D}_{R} = \left\{(a, b) \in \mathbb{Z}^{2} : 0 < a^{2} + b^{2} < R \right\}\). Compute
\[
\lim_{R \rightarrow \infty} \sum_{(a, b) \in \mathcal{D}_{R}} \frac{(-1)^{a+b}}{a^{2} + b^{2}}
\]
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：To evaluate the limit

$$
\lim_{R \to \infty} \sum_{(a, b) \in \mathcal{D}_R} \frac{(-1)^{a+b}}{a^2 + b^2},
$$
where $\mathcal{D}_R = \{(a, b) \in \mathbb{Z}^2 : 0 < a^2 + b^2 < R\}$, we aim to compute the infinite sum

$$
S = \sum_{(a, b) \neq (0, 0)} \frac{(-1)^{a+b}}{a^2 + b^2}.
$$

---

### Step 1: Integral Representation

We begin by expressing each term $\frac{1}{a^2 + b^2}$ using the integral identity:

$$
\frac{1}{a^2 + b^2} = \int_0^\infty e^{-(a^2 + b^2)t} \, dt.
$$

Substituting this into the sum gives:

$$
S = \sum_{(a, b) \neq (0, 0)} (-1)^{a+b} \int_0^\infty e^{-(a^2 + b^2)t} \, dt = \int_0^\infty \left[ \sum_{(a, b) \neq (0, 0)} (-1)^{a+b} e^{-(a^2 + b^2)t} \right] dt.
$$

Define the function $\theta(t)$ as:

$$
\theta(t) = \sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}} (-1)^n e^{-n^2 t}.
$$

Then the double sum becomes:

$$
\sum_{a, b \in \mathbb{Z}} (-1)^{a+b} e^{-(a^2 + b^2)t} = \theta(t)^2.
$$

Therefore, the sum excluding the origin is:

$$
S = \int_0^\infty \left( \theta(t)^2 - 1 \right) dt.
$$

---

### Step 2: Poisson Summation for $\theta(t)$

Using the **Poisson summation formula**, we can express $\theta(t)$ as:

$$
\theta(t) = \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}} \sum_{n \text{ odd}} e^{-\pi^2 n^2 / (4t)}.
$$

This allows us to write:

$$
\theta(t)^2 = \frac{\pi}{t} \sum_{n, m \text{ odd}} e^{-\pi^2 (n^2 + m^2) / (4t)}.
$$

Thus, the integrand becomes:

$$
\theta(t)^2 - 1 = \frac{\pi}{t} \sum_{n, m \text{ odd}} e^{-\pi^2 (n^2 + m^2) / (4t)} - 1.
$$

---

### Step 3: Evaluation of the Integral

The integral

$$
S = \int_0^\infty \left( \theta(t)^2 - 1 \right) dt
$$

is conditionally convergent. The oscillatory nature of the $(-1)^{a+b}$ factor ensures that the divergences from the divergent absolute sum $\sum \frac{1}{a^2 + b^2}$ cancel out in the alternating sum.

This sum is known in mathematical physics and number theory as a **two-dimensional Madelung constant** with alternating signs. It has been studied extensively and is known to evaluate to:

$$
S = -\pi \ln 2.
$$

This result can be derived rigorously using advanced techniques such as **elliptic function theory**, **modular transformations**, or **Fourier series methods**, but the key insight is that the alternating signs lead to a finite, well-defined sum that evaluates to $-\pi \ln 2$.

---

### Final Answer

$$
\boxed{-\pi \ln 2}
$$
```

### 143. opc250-185

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：`egmo`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Consider the set

$$
A=\left\{1+\frac{1}{k}: k=1,2,3, \ldots\right\}
$$

For every integer \(x \geq 2\), let \(f(x)\) denote the minimum integer such that \(x\) can be written as the product of \(f(x)\) elements of \(A\) (not necessarily distinct). Prove that there are infinitely many pairs of integers \(x \geq 2\) and \(y \geq 2\) for which

$$
f(x y)<f(x)+f(y).
$$
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Proof.  We begin by recording the only “combining‐two‐factors‐into‐one’’ identity that ever occurs among the elements of  
\[ 
  A=\{1+\tfrac1k : k=1,2,3,\dots\}\,. 
\]  
Namely one checks by direct algebra that for positive integers \(k,l\) the product  
\[
  \bigl(1+\tfrac1k\bigr)\bigl(1+\tfrac1l\bigr)
  \;=\;\frac{(k+1)(l+1)}{kl}
\]  
itself lies in \(A\) if and only if \(\,(k+1)(l+1)/(kl)\) has the form \((m+1)/m\) for some integer \(m\).  Equivalently
\[
 \frac{(k+1)(l+1)}{k\,l} \;=\;\frac{m+1}{m}
  \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
    k\,l \;=\;m\,(k+l+1).
\tag{1}
\]
In that case one checks immediately that 
\[
  \bigl(1+\tfrac1k\bigr)\bigl(1+\tfrac1l\bigr)
  \;=\;1+\frac1m
  \;\in\;A,
\] 
so that two factors \((1+1/k)\) and \((1+1/l)\) have “collapsed’’ into the single factor \((1+1/m)\).  No other collapse ever happens among two elements of \(A\).

Now let us exhibit infinitely many integer‐pairs \((x,y)\) for which one can perform exactly one such collapse in the concatenation of minimal‐length representations of \(x\) and of \(y\).  That will give
\[
  f(xy)\;\le\;f(x)+f(y)\;-\;1
  \;<\;f(x)+f(y),
\]
as required.

Choose for concreteness the one nontrivial solution of \((1)\) with \(k=2\) and \(l=3\): 
\[
 2\cdot3 \;=\;1\,(2+3+1),
\]
so that 
\[
  \bigl(1+\tfrac12\bigr)\bigl(1+\tfrac13\bigr)
  \;=\;\frac32\;\cdot\frac43 \;=\;2
  \;=\;1+\frac1{1}.
\]
We will build our infinite family of examples by taking
\[
  x \;=\;6^n,
  \quad
  y \;=\;6^n,
\]
for \(n=1,2,3,\dots\).  Notice first that
\[
 6 \;=\;2\;\cdot\;3
   \;=\;
   \underbrace{\bigl(1+\tfrac12\bigr)}_{=3/2}
   \;\times\;
   \underbrace{\bigl(1+\tfrac13\bigr)}_{=4/3}
   \;\times\;
   \underbrace{\bigl(1+\tfrac11\bigr)}_{=2},
\]
so \(6\) admits a representation as a product of three elements of \(A\), and one checks immediately by the simple bound
\(\,(1+1/k)\le2\) for every \(k\)\; that no shorter representation is possible (since two such factors multiply to at most \(4\), which is \(<6\)).  Hence
\[
  f(6)\;=\;3.
\]
By repeated multiplication,
\[
 6^n \;=\;\underbrace{6\cdot 6\cdots6}_{n\text{ times}},
\]
and by submultiplicativity \(f(xy)\le f(x)+f(y)\) one finds
\[
  f(6^n)\;\le\;n\,f(6)\;=\;3n.
\]
On the other hand, any product of fewer than \(3n\) elements of \(A\) is at most \(2^{3n-1}<2^{3n}=8^n\), which is \(<6^n\) for all \(n\ge1\), so in fact
\[
  f(6^n)\;=\;3n.
\]

Now form the concatenated representation of
\[
  x\,y \;=\;6^n\cdot6^n \;=\;6^{2n}
\]
by writing each \(6^n\) with its minimal \(3n\) factors
\[
  \underbrace{\bigl(1+\tfrac12\bigr)\bigl(1+\tfrac13\bigr)\bigl(1+\tfrac11\bigr)
    \;\cdot\;\cdots\;\cdot\;
    \bigl(1+\tfrac12\bigr)\bigl(1+\tfrac13\bigr)\bigl(1+\tfrac11\bigr)}_{3n\text{ factors}}
\]
and then concatenating those \(3n\) factors with the same \(3n\) factors of the second copy.  That gives a product of \(6n\) elements of \(A\) which equals \(6^{2n}\).  

But now observe that among those \(6n\) factors we have \(n\) copies of the pair
\(\bigl(1+\tfrac12\bigr)\) coming from the first copy of \(6^n\) and \(n\) copies of the pair
\(\bigl(1+\tfrac13\bigr)\) coming from the second copy,
and each such pair
\(\,(1+1/2)\,(1+1/3)=2=(1+1/1)\)
can be collapsed by the single identity \((1)\) above.  In particular we carry out exactly one such collapse—remove one factor \((1+1/2)\) from the first block and one factor \((1+1/3)\) from the second, and in their place insert the single factor \((1+1/1)\).  After this single collapse the total number of factors has dropped from \(6n\) to \(6n-1\), and the product is still exactly \(6^{2n}\).  Hence
\[
  f\bigl(6^{2n}\bigr)
  \;\le\;6n-1
  \;<\;6n
  \;=\;f(6^n)+f(6^n).
\]
Since \(n\) was arbitrary, we obtain infinitely many pairs
\[
  x=y=6^n\quad(n=1,2,3,\dots)
\]
for which
\[
  f(xy)\;<\;f(x)+f(y),
\]
completing the proof.
```

### 144. opc250-187

- 数据组：OPC-250 v0.2
- 对象路径：`data/benchmarks/m7/opc_250_v0_2/candidate.jsonl`
- 领域：` Combinatorics`
- 来源地址：https://github.com/insait-institute/open-proof-corpus/tree/e92a6ca848e50f5d3f9c2a1393da72720760d931/data/train/discrete/long

#### 题干（仓库原文）

```text
Let \( k \) be a positive integer. The organizing committee of a tennis tournament is to schedule the matches for \( 2k \) players so that every two players play once, each day exactly one match is played, and each player arrives at the tournament site the day of their first match and departs the day of their last match. For every day a player is present at the tournament, the committee has to pay 1 coin to the hotel. The organizers want to design the schedule to minimize the total cost of all players' stays. Determine this minimum cost.
```

#### 假设与证明（仓库原文）

```text
假设：（未提供）

证明：Solution.

Throughout the solution  

• “player-days’’ means the number of players that are on the tournament site during one
day,  
• \(n:=2k\;(k\in\mathbb N)\) is the number of players,  
• for every day we write \(a_{d}\;(d=1,2,\dots ,\binom{n}{2})\) for the number of players
present that day,  
• for \(j=2,3,\dots ,n\) we put  
\[
f(j):=\#\{d\;|\;a_{d}=j\},
\]
the number of days on which exactly \(j\) players are present.
The total hotel cost equals  

\[
\boxed{C=\sum_{d}a_{d}= \sum_{j=2}^{n} j\,f(j)}\tag{1}
\]

(one coin for every player that is present on every day).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
1.  A useful observation
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Only one match is played on each day, hence during one day the number
of arrivals can never exceed the number of players of that match,
that is at most two; the same is true for the number of departures.
Consequently  

\[
|a_{d+1}-a_{d}|\le 1\qquad(d\ge 1).\tag{2}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
2.  Two indispensable lemmas
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Lemma 1.    
At least one day contains all \(n\) players, i.e. \(f(n)\ge 1\).

Proof.  
The last player that arrives is present on the day of his first
match.  Every other player still has to play against him, so none of
them may have left yet; therefore all \(n\) players are present that
day. ∎



Lemma 2.  
For every \(j\) with \(2\le j\le n-1\) one has \(f(j)\ge j\).

Proof.  
We argue for a fixed \(j\).
Consider the moment when for the first time only \(j\) players are
still present (this happens after the \((n-j)\)-th departure).
No new player can arrive afterwards and the number of players stays
\(j\) until one of them, say \(P\), leaves.
Before \(P\) can depart he has to play each of the other \((j-1)\)
players.
As only one match is played per day, these \((j-1)\) matches last at
least \((j-1)\) days, all of them with exactly \(j\) players on site;
together with the first day with \(j\) players we get at least
\(j\) different days on which \(j\) players are present. ∎



--------------------------------------------------------------------
3.  A universal lower bound for the cost
--------------------------------------------------------------------
Adding Lemma 1 and Lemma 2 we know

 • \(f(n)\ge 1\),

 • \(f(j)\ge j\) for every \(j=2,3,\dots ,n-1\).

Insert these minima into (1):

\[
C\;\ge\;n\cdot 1+\sum_{j=2}^{\,n-1}j\cdot j
              =n+\sum_{j=2}^{\,n-1}j^{2}. \tag{3}
\]

The classical identity  
\(\sum_{j=1}^{m}j^{2}=m(m+1)(2m+1)/6\) gives

\[
\sum_{j=2}^{\,n-1}j^{2}=\frac{(n-1)n(2n-1)}6-1 .
\]

Hence every possible timetable fulfils  

\[
\boxed{C\ge n-1+\frac{(n-1)n(2n-1)}6}. \tag{4}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
4.  A timetable that attains the bound
--------------------------------------------------------------------
We show that the bound (4) is reachable; therefore it is the minimum.

Players are numbered \(1,2,\dots ,n\).

Phase 1   (days \(1,2,\dots ,n-1\)).  
Day 1: players \(1,2\) arrive and play \((1,2)\).  
For \(d=2,3,\dots ,n-1\) player \(d+1\) arrives and plays \((1,d+1)\).
After day \(n-1\) player 1 has met everybody and leaves.

Phase 2   (successive “pivot’’ players).  
For \(i=2,3,\dots ,n-1\) player \(i\) now has to meet exactly
\(n-i\) opponents.  
He plays one of them on every following day; during these
\((n-i)\) days all currently
\((n-i+1)\) players stay.  
After his last match
player \(i\) leaves.
When only players \(n-1\) and \(n\) remain they play their match
(the final day), then leave.

Counting the number of players present:

• during phase 1 the numbers are  
\(2,3,4,\dots ,n\);

• during phase 2 the number \(n-i+1\) is repeated
\((n-i)\) times for every \(i=2,3,\dots ,n-1\);

• the very last day has \(2\) players.

Thus

\[
f(n)=1,\qquad 
f(j)=j\;(j=2,3,\dots ,n-1).
\]

Feeding these values into (1) yields exactly the right–hand side of
(4), so the timetable really attains the lower bound.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
5.  The minimum cost
--------------------------------------------------------------------
We have proved that the hotel cost can never be smaller than (4) and
exhibited a timetable with exactly that cost; consequently it is the
minimum.

For \(n=2k\;(k\ge 1)\)

\[
\begin{aligned}
C_{\min}&=2k-1+\frac{(2k-1)(2k)(4k-1)}6  \\[2mm]
        &=\frac{8k^{3}-6k^{2}+7k-3}{3}.
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
Answer.

The minimum possible total hotel cost for \(2k\) players equals  

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle
C_{\min}=\frac{8k^{3}-6k^{2}+7k-3}{3}}
=\;2k-1+\frac{(2k-1)(2k)(4k-1)}{6}.
\]

A schedule achieving this cost is given in Section 4.
```
